\author{Sajjad~Khan%
\thanks{S.~Khan is an independent researcher (e-mail: sajjadanwar200@gmail.com).}%
\thanks{Artifact: \url{https://github.com/sajjadanwar0/mac-consistency-mono}, tag \texttt{v4.1-tse}, cut by a gate that refuses while any cell of this text is marked pending: \TLA{} models, Verus proofs, the runtime, every harness and the committed traces behind every table; \texttt{reproduce.sh} re-derives each figure, and every stated number is in \texttt{CLAIMS.json} or regenerated into \texttt{claims\_results.tex} from a committed receipt. A supplement (\S\S\,A--W) holds raw tables and proofs omitted for length.}}

\markboth{IEEE Transactions (submitted)}{Khan: Mechanized Anomaly Predicates, Verified Disciplines, and the Residue They Leave in Multi-Agent LLM Systems}

\IEEEtitleabstractindextext{%
\begin{abstract}
An agent's step in a multi-agent LLM system is a transaction whose compute phase is an inference call of unbounded latency, during which the state it read is neither locked nor re-validated. We give the regime a mechanized consistency theory and test what it buys. Four classical conditions become history predicates---stale generation, phantom tool, causal cascade, tool-effect reordering---with TLC witnesses; TLAPS proves that stale-generation freedom implies commit-order read consistency under three hypotheses, which gates check on the committed records; bounded model checking realizes all sixteen points of their exclusion lattice; and Verus verifies one chain of disciplines whose verified files are byte-identical to the runtime that was measured. The empirical result is what commit-time isolation cannot reach. On a released LangGraph runtime with live models, its store instrumented only to stamp a clock, read-set validation removes every commit grounded in a superseded store read, yet the booking contradicts the final trip date in $1$--$65\%$ of sessions per API family and arm, because the revision lands after the commit---a harm that occurs in correct sequential executions. We define it as a predicate on effects and their recovered bases, observe that under an open-world store it can only be owed, and test a basis ledger that registers the obligation with the superseding write against a simulated world with refusals and a downstream consumer: the ledger's report and the world agree in $\LWLedgerAgree$ sessions, a compensator that misreported was caught only by the world check, and a consumer still acts on the stale booking until the compensation's model call completes (or finds none, under a compensator that cancels first). A released tool executor commits an order-dependent pipeline out of order on $22$ of $30$ turns of one backbone while its transcript shows it in order. Per sound committed operation, snapshot isolation costs $+0.4$--$11.1\%$ in tokens and pessimistic locking up to $+97\%$; per session, a model told the truth about a refusal makes $2.2$--$4.2\times$ the calls on the contended workloads when the refusal names no agent and $1.1$--$2.3\times$ when it does; a census of $98$ public LangGraph repositories finds the precondition's statically visible shape, a co-scheduled reader and writer of one last-value channel, confirmed nowhere. From these we derive four concrete requirements for agent frameworks. Every number carries its receipt and its assurance level.
\end{abstract}

\begin{IEEEkeywords}
Multi-agent systems, large language models, memory consistency, isolation levels, formal verification, TLA+, Verus.
\end{IEEEkeywords}}

\maketitle
\IEEEdisplaynontitleabstractindextext
\IEEEpeerreviewmaketitle

% ============================================================================
% sec/01-intro.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

A multi-agent LLM system shares state: one agent writes a plan that another
reads, a tool registry is consulted at planning time and called at execution
time, a supervisor retracts a decision a worker has already acted on. Each
agent's step has the shape of a transaction---read, compute, commit---with one
difference from every setting in which transactions have been studied: the
compute phase is an inference call that takes seconds to minutes, during
which the read set is neither locked nor cheaply re-validated. Deployed
frameworks place this phase inside a pinned snapshot (LangGraph's superstep),
a re-rendered context (Letta) or a message queue (AutoGen), and none states
what guarantee the shared state carries across it.

\subsection{A failure, constructed and then observed}
\label{sec:intro-example}
Two agents share a trip record. A flight agent reads ``trip date $=$ 14 June,''
drafts a reservation over a model call, and books it. During that call a second
agent, acting for the user, moves the trip to 21 June. The flight agent commits
a 14 June booking beside a 21 June trip date. No component failed; the history
is even serializable, with the flight agent first and the revision after. What
it is not is serializable \emph{in the order in which its operations
committed}: the revision committed first, and a flight agent executing after
it would have read 21 June (\cref{fig:window}).

We ran this scenario on a released LangGraph runtime under live models, its
store instrumented only to stamp a logical clock
(\cref{sec:live-a1}). With the second agent started inside the first agent's
generation window, the framework committed the stale booking in every session
in which the revision landed inside the window---$\LiveGptVanillaAOne\%$ and
$\LiveHaikuVanillaAOne\%$ of sessions on two API models---and read-set
validation at commit removed every such firing. The same data record a fact
the predicate does not see: when the revision lands \emph{after} the commit,
the booking contradicts the final trip date just the same, and no isolation
discipline can prevent it. We report both numbers throughout, because their
distance is the distance between a consistency predicate and the harm it is
meant to track; the rates are properties of the race we opened ($150$\,ms
offset; swept in \cref{fig:offset}) and carry no prevalence reading.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{What this paper is not claiming.} A census of
\CensusRepos{} public LangGraph repositories (\cref{sec:prevalence}) finds
the statically visible form of \A1's precondition---a reader and a writer of
one last-value channel scheduled together inside a graph---confirmed in none
of \CensusSites{} graph sites, eight unresolved; the cross-thread store the
failure above shares is not visible to a static front end and is not counted.
We take the census at face value:
public LangGraph code is mostly sequential, and four of five frameworks we
probed close the window by construction. The regime studied is the one in
which contention is designed in (parallel fan-out over a shared store,
several agents for one user, a registry mutated in flight); the contribution
is not that the anomalies are common but mechanized definitions, disciplines
priced per unit of sound work, and an account of what each leaves behind.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.62cm,y=0.52cm,font=\scriptsize,>=Latex]
  % time axis
  \draw[->] (0,0) -- (9.0,0) node[right]{$t$};
  \foreach \x/\l in {0.4/0,2.4/1,5.2/2,8.0/3} {\draw (\x,0.08)--(\x,-0.08) node[below]{\l};}
  % flight agent
  \node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.1,2.2) {flight\\agent};

  \node at (4.2,2.2) {generation phase (inference)};
  \draw[->,thick] (0.4,1.8) -- (0.4,0.3) node[midway,left]{read $c$};
  \node[anchor=south] at (0.4,2.6) {$\rt=0$};
  \draw[->,thick] (8.0,1.8) -- (8.0,0.3) node[midway,right]{commit $c'$};
  \node[anchor=south] at (8.0,2.6) {$\wt=3$};
  % date agent
  \node[anchor=east,align=right] at (-0.1,1.0) {date\\agent};
  \draw[fill=black!20,draw=black!60] (2.4,0.7) rectangle (5.2,1.3);
  \draw[->,thick] (5.2,0.7) -- (5.2,0.3) node[midway,right]{write $c$};

\subsection{Why existing theory does not operationalize these failures}
\label{sec:why-classical-fails}
Hardware models~\cite{Lamport1979SC,HerlihyWing1990} presuppose bounded
latency and static access sets; database isolation
theory~\cite{BerensonEtAl1995,Adya1999,AdyaLiskovOneil2000} supplies the
anomaly-by-anomaly method we follow but holds the read set under lock or
snapshot for the transaction's lifetime; replicated-storage
consistency~\cite{LloydEtAl2011COPS} treats operations as discrete events
with summarizable signatures. The agent regime has unbounded generation
latency, a mutable tool registry and irreversible effects, so the
cascading-abort machinery of database recovery, which assumes effects can be
undone, is what fails.

\subsection{Contributions}
\label{sec:contributions}
We claim no new concurrency theory; each anomaly below is a classical one,
and we say which. The contribution is the operational port of four of them to
agent runtimes, its mechanization at stated assurance levels, and an
evaluation that measures the gap between each predicate and the harm it
tracks, including the part of the harm that is not a concurrency anomaly.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(C1) A catalog with classical names, and what it
leaves out} (\cref{sec:anomalies}). Four history predicates in
\TLA{}---stale generation (\A1), phantom tool (\A2), causal cascade (\A3),
tool-effect reordering (\A6)---each with a TLC witness and its classical
counterpart: a failed backward validation~\cite{KungRobinson1981OCC}, a
dangling capability reference, recoverability with externalization as the
commit point~\cite{BernsteinHadzilacosGoodman1987}, and order preservation
of external effects. \Cref{thm:corc} (TLAPS, \TlapsCommitOrder{}
obligations) places \A1-freedom among the classical classes exactly, a
conditional guarantee whose hypotheses (per-agent non-overlap,
read-before-commit, faithful records) are stated, checked on every committed
record, and violated by one deployed construct we name. Two predicates stand
outside the catalog, each for a stated reason: the \emph{residue predicate}
\Rp{}---an irreversible effect is out while ordinary later writes have revised
the state it was derived from; not an anomaly (it fires in correct sequential
executions) but the harm no commit-time discipline reaches, decided on the
records by argument provenance; on the live study \A1 and \Rp{} partition the
outcome column (\cref{sec:residue})---and the \emph{duplication predicate}
$D$---one node's irreversible effect out twice, the shape a deployed
resumption path produces, with its witness and the guard that excludes it
(\cref{sec:dup}).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(C2) A lattice, realized at stated bounds}
(\cref{sec:lattice}). The $2^4$ exclusion lattice is trivial; what is not is
that every point is inhabited by a checked runtime configuration that excludes
exactly its anomalies (\LatticeRuns{} TLC runs, no mismatch, at two agents,
two cells, two operations and, for the safety half, two values\ifnum\LatticeAOneMaxOpsThreeAvailable=1 ; the
\textsc{NoA1} half rechecked at three operations\fi), that one chain
$L_0\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq L_4$ has a verified realizing runtime at every
level, and that the basis ledger composes with every point without
disturbing its verdict (\LatticeThirtyTwoRuns{} further runs).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(C3) Verified disciplines, verified detectors, and
exactly which bytes were measured} (\cref{sec:mechanization}). Under two
enumerated axioms and three enumerated stubs, with no \texttt{assume} or
\texttt{admit}: detectors sound and complete against their specifications,
two concurrent $L_1$ stores proved window-free under a verified
reader--writer lock, and $L_2$, $L_3$ and $L_4$ runtimes whose verified files
are, byte for byte, the files the runtime compiles (a gate checks the
identity) and the bytes measured\ifnum\ToolNodeVerifiedAvailable=1\else{}
($L_3$'s replay through its bytes is \Pending{step 15})\fi.
\ifnum\VerifiedScorerAvailable=1 The \A1 verdict of every table is the
verified detector executing, behind one interning wrapper in the trust base;
every published cell was rescored through it (\VerifiedScorerCells{} cells,
\VerifiedScorerTraces{} traces, \VerifiedScorerProblems{}
disagreements).\else The \A1 verdict of every table is the verified detector
executing, behind one interning wrapper in the trust base, gated against a
hand-written port on every cell; the gate's last pass rescored
\VerifiedScorerCells{} cells (\VerifiedScorerTraces{} traces) and exited
non-zero on \VerifiedScorerProblems{} per-cell refusal sidecars it read as
op-records, so its clean receipt is \Pending{runner step 14 rerun}.\fi{}
\Cref{tab:assurance} labels every claim with its assurance level (checked
model, verified decision procedure, verified bytes, unverified port, open) and
names each correspondence between \TLA{} definition, Verus restatement,
executed code and scorer, with its hypothesis and gate; of \VerusCurated{}
obligations, \VerusLoadBearing{} bear a deployed or measured artifact
(supplement \S\,S).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(C4) An evaluation scored on outcomes, not flags,
with its own confounds measured} (\cref{sec:eval}). For each discipline we
report the anomaly it excludes, the harm that remains, and the price: on the
live LangGraph study the $L_1$ residue is \Rp{} in $1$--$65\%$ of sessions
per API family and arm (pooled over three interleaved runs; $0$--$73$ of
$100$ in single runs), in two implementations of $L_1$ between which the
replications detect no difference; against a simulated world the
ledger's report and the world agree in $\LWatomicLedgerAgree$ sessions, where a
first compensator had misreported $1$--$10\%$ (\cref{sec:ledger-world}); output
commit removes exposure exactly when the review precedes the release; per
sound committed operation, snapshot isolation costs $+0.4$--$11.1\%$ and
pessimistic locking up to $+97\%$ in tokens, and, per session,
$2.2$--$4.2\times$ the model calls under a refusal that names no agent and
$1.1$--$2.3\times$ under one that does (\cref{sec:cost}). Where the design limits what a
difference can mean we quantify the confound on the receipts.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{(C5) Where the preconditions occur} (\cref{sec:prevalence}).
A census of \CensusRepos{} public LangGraph repositories (\CensusSites{} graph
sites) finds no confirmed co-scheduled reader and writer of a last-value
channel (eight sites unresolved; the static front end cannot resolve the
routing of sites in about half the repositories)---a
negative result for \A1's structural precondition, which we report as
one---while
a released tool executor dispatches a model-emitted, order-dependent pipeline
concurrently on $\ToolNodeGptBatched/\ToolNodeGptBatched$ turns of one backbone
and never on another, which is the case for \A6. \textbf{(C6)} Four
requirements for agent frameworks follow, each priced (\cref{sec:conclusion}).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{What this paper does not establish.} No
production prevalence for any anomaly: every occurrence figure is a race our
harness opened or a rate on our own workloads, and the census measures how
public code is written, not how often it runs. The instrumentation and the
Python harnesses are not verified (\cref{sec:trust}); the refinement of the
deployed stores to their abstract machines is open; a verified arm's zero is
a theorem executing, not evidence.

% ============================================================================
% sec/02-model.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Runtime model}
\label{sec:model}

A system consists of a finite set of agents $A$, a shared memory $M$ mapping
cells to values with a distinguished sentinel $\mathrm{NULL}$, and a shared
registry $R$ of available tools. An agent executes a sequence of
\emph{operations}, each in three phases: a \emph{read} phase that captures the
values of a chosen subset of cells (the operation's $\rs$) together with the
registry contents ($\rreg$), stamping the read with the current log length as
$\rt$; a \emph{generation} phase, dominated by inference latency and not
treated as instantaneous; and a \emph{write} phase that atomically appends a
record to the log, installing the planned writes ($\ws$, $\wv$), the selected
tool ($\pt$) and the registry at commit ($\wreg$), stamped $\wt$. The log
length is a logical clock~\cite{Lamport1978TimeClocks}; a history $h$ is the
log, $h\in\mathrm{Seq}(\fld{OpRecord})$ (\texttt{Memory.tla}).

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Store axiom.} A read returns the latest committed
value at or before its read time (stamps are log lengths, so no write
shares a stamp with a read; ``after'' is \cref{def:a1}'s strict inequality).
TLC confirms this and the two hypotheses below as invariants of
\texttt{Memory.tla} (\CommitOrderMemoryStates{} reachable states at the
checked bounds); gates check all three on the committed records
(\cref{sec:a1}).

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Per-agent non-overlap; read before commit.} Two
operations of one agent do not overlap in $[\rt,\wt]$; every operation reads
strictly before it commits. Both are invariants of the model, and both are
load-bearing for \cref{thm:corc} and for the detector transcription of
\cref{sec:verus-detectors}. A framework that runs one node as several parallel
instances under one name (LangGraph's \texttt{Send} fan-out) violates the first
and is outside the model; \cref{sec:prevalence} counts how often that shape
appears in public code.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Determinism is not assumed by the theorems.} Every
mechanized result ranges over recorded histories with write values free; under
sampling a value mismatch need not change the downstream decision, so the
\A1 detector is a sound candidate screen whose over-reporting
\cref{sec:live-a1} measures (materiality), the stance of durable-execution
engines~\cite{BurckhardtEtAl2021Durable}.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Atomic commit is at the record level.} External
effects are outside the store and are governed separately: their order by
\A6 and $L_3$, their release relative to a still-retractable basis by \A3 and
$L_2$. At-most-once externalization is a hypothesis of this model; it is
stated as the predicate $D$ of \cref{sec:dup}, checked on every committed
record (\HypDuplicateEffects{} duplicate emissions among \HypExternalEffects{}
externalized effects), and violated by one deployed resumption path we
reproduce.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{No replication; no one runtime.} The memory is one
logical store (split view under replication, \A4, is in supplement \S\,J);
LangGraph is driven through the model live (\cref{sec:live-a1}), three other
runtimes are placed informally (supplement \S\,D).

% ============================================================================
% sec/03-catalog.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{The anomaly catalog}
\label{sec:anomalies}

Four anomaly predicates over histories are formalized in \TLA{}
(\texttt{Anomalies.tla}); each has a TLC witness at $|A|{=}2$,
$|\fld{Cells}|\le 2$, $\fld{MaxOps}\le 4$, reproduced by the artifact's
\texttt{reproduce.sh}. Each predicate's classical counterpart and the
regime feature that forces the port are named in its subsection and
tabulated in supplement \S\,R. The numbering
($A_1, A_2, A_3, A_6$) is kept from the public artifact (an earlier $A_5$
was subsumed by \A1); \cref{sec:residue} defines, outside the catalog, the
predicate for what the four leave behind.


\subsection{\A1: stale generation}
\label{sec:a1}
\begin{definition}[Stale generation]
\label{def:a1}
A history $h$ exhibits \emph{stale generation} when there exist
$i\neq j$ with $h[i].\fld{agent}\neq h[j].\fld{agent}$ and
$c\in h[i].\rs\cap h[j].\ws$ such that
\[
  \begin{gathered}
  h[i].\rt < h[j].\wt < h[i].\wt\\
  \text{and}\quad h[i].\rv[c] \neq h[j].\wv[c].
  \end{gathered}
\]
\end{definition}

\noindent\emph{Witness.} TLC finds a five-state counterexample: $a_2$ reads
$c_1=\mathrm{NULL}$ at $\rt=0$; $a_1$ commits $c_1=v_1$ at $\wt=1$; $a_2$
commits at $\wt=2$ with $\rv[c_1]=\mathrm{NULL}$.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Classical placement.} The minimal witness is a single
read--write antidependency from $i$ to $j$, serializable in the order $i;j$,
and none of Adya's proscribed phenomena~\cite{Adya1999}; TLC exhibits such a
history (claim \texttt{A1OnlyOnNonSerializable}, violated as expected). \A1
is therefore not an isolation anomaly in the classical sense. Its
\emph{absence} is nevertheless a classical correctness class, and we state
which one at the level at which the theorem holds.

\begin{definition}[Commit-order read consistency]
\label{def:corc}
A history is \emph{commit-order read consistent} when every operation read, for
every cell in its read set, exactly the value it would have read had it
executed atomically at its own commit point: for all $i$ and $c\in h[i].\rs$,
$h[i].\rv[c]$ is the value of the latest write to $c$ with
$\wt<h[i].\wt$, or $\mathrm{NULL}$ if there is none.
\end{definition}

Writes are installed in commit order in any case, so \cref{def:corc} is
value-level equivalence, on the reads, to the serial execution in commit
order: the value-level analogue of Weikum and Vossen's commit-order-preserving
serializability~\cite{WeikumVossen2001}, not that conflict-based condition
itself; under value-unique writes the two coincide on reads (view
equivalence~\cite{Papadimitriou1979}).

\begin{theorem}[\A1-freedom implies commit-order read consistency]
\label{thm:corc}
If $h$ satisfies the store axiom, read-before-commit and per-agent
non-overlap and has no \A1 witness, it is commit-order read consistent.
\end{theorem}

The proof (\texttt{CommitOrder.tla}; TLAPS, \TlapsCommitOrder{} obligations,
no omitted step) is Kung and Robinson's backward-validation argument run
against our definitions: if some operation's read of $c$ disagrees with the
latest write to $c$ before its commit, that write lies inside the operation's
window, was made by another agent (one agent's operations do not overlap), and
wrote a different value---an \A1 witness. Fifteen TLC verdicts
(\texttt{check\_commitorder.sh}) fix what the statement says, and all
reproduce: over all \CommitOrderHistories{} histories of length at most
three (one cell, one value) the implication holds, is not vacuous, and fails
when either droppable hypothesis is removed; the relational definitions equal \texttt{Memory.tla}'s
and a literal serial replay; the three hypotheses are invariants of the
operational model over \CommitOrderMemoryStates{} reachable states; and the
converse fails in exactly one shape, a value overwritten and then restored
inside the window. The predicates nest strictly:
\[
  \neg\text{CORC} \;\Longrightarrow\; A_1 \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{validation fails}.
\]
\emph{Reach.} The theorem is a conditional guarantee over faithful histories
that satisfy its three hypotheses; it says nothing about serializability of
an application, about effects, about reads the instrumentation did not
declare, or about a runtime that violates per-agent non-overlap (LangGraph's
\texttt{Send} fan-out, \cref{sec:model}). On the committed data all three hypotheses
hold where they can be checked (\texttt{hypothesis\_gates.py}, failing on
the first violation): \HypSameAgentOverlaps{} same-agent overlapping windows
and \HypReadBeforeCommitViolations{} reads not before their commit in
\HypRecords{} operation records over \HypTraces{} traces (ledger
registration records are not operations), and \HypStoreAxiomViolations{}
store-axiom violations over \HypStoreAxiomReads{} declared reads in the
\HypStoreAxiomTraces{} traces that record their store (compensation records,
which read nothing, carry a synthesized read time one tick before their
commit and pass trivially). The \HypStoreAxiomConvertedCampaigns{} campaigns
converted from transcripts and tool logs are not taken at a store boundary
and fail it---of their \HypStoreAxiomConvertedReads{} reconstructed reads,
$3{,}289$ disagree with the latest visible write and $1{,}226$ read an
unrecorded cell (reported, not gated)---which is why they supply no rate of
this paper's tables: the \HypPublishedCampaigns{} campaigns behind them are
store-instrumented (\HypPublishedConvertedCampaigns{} converted; gated), so
every \A1 verdict here is taken on records the hypotheses were checked on.
The converse shape is a false positive of $L_1$, priced
in discarded generations; on the live records it occurs in \HypRestored{} of
\HypAOneWitnesses{} \A1 witness pairs, and cannot on these workloads, whose
revisions are monotone. Two corollaries are mechanized with the theorem: the
classical lost update (P4 of Berenson et al.~\cite{BerensonEtAl1995}) is an
\A1 witness whose stale reader also writes the cell it read, so $L_1$
excludes it; a \emph{blind} overwrite---a write by an operation that never
read the cell---is outside isolation theory and the catalog, and is the kind
of the one in-the-wild bug we reproduce (supplement \S\,Q) and of a
revision landing \emph{after} the reader's commit (\cref{sec:live-a1}).

\subsection{\A2: phantom tool}
\label{sec:a2}
\begin{definition}[Phantom tool]
\label{def:a2}
$h$ exhibits a \emph{phantom tool} when for some $i$,
\[
  \begin{gathered}
  h[i].\pt\neq\mathrm{NULL},\\
  h[i].\pt\in h[i].\rreg,\\
  h[i].\pt\notin h[i].\wreg.
  \end{gathered}
\]
\end{definition}
An agent selects a tool from the registry it read; the registry mutates
during generation; the commit emits a call to a capability that no longer
exists. TLC's witness has four states. \Cref{def:a2} is stated over
identifiers; the Verus restatement of \cref{sec:l4} maps identifiers to
signatures and fires additionally when a tool is re-signed under its old
name. The two coincide under the encoding the executable dispatcher uses
(re-signing removes the old pair and inserts a fresh one), which
\cref{tab:assurance} records as an encoding, not a proof. A hallucinated call to a tool
never in $\rreg$ is not \A2 and not in the catalog.

\subsection{\A3: causal cascade}
\label{sec:a3}
When a compensating (saga-style) runtime retracts an operation, any
operation that read a value it produced is grounded on a basis no surviving
commit supports. The classical remedy, cascading abort, flags the dependent
aborted; if the dependent's tool effects are already out, the flag changes
nothing that reached the world, so the cataloged predicate is stated over
externalization.

\begin{definition}[Causal cascade]
\label{def:a3}
Let each record carry $\fld{aborted}$ and $\fld{externalized}$ flags and a
predecessor set $\fld{preds}$ (the operations whose committed writes it
observed, transitively closed). $h$ exhibits a \emph{causal cascade} when for
some $j,p$:
\[
  h[j].\fld{externalized}\;\wedge\;p\in h[j].\fld{preds}\;\wedge\;h[p].\fld{aborted}.
\]
\end{definition}
The surviving-dependent form, $\neg h[j].\fld{aborted}\wedge p\in
h[j].\fld{preds}\wedge h[p].\fld{aborted}$, is kept beside it as
\textsc{CascadeUnpropagated}; a cascading abort falsifies that form by
flagging $j$ after $j$'s effects are out, and \cref{sec:l2-eval} measures the
separation on runnable arms.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Prevention is output commit, and output commit has a
residue.} Under \cref{def:a3} a runtime must hold an operation's effects
(output commit, in the sense of optimistic
recovery~\cite{StromYemini1985OptimisticRecovery}) until
every operation in its closure has released its own, and must refuse to
retract an operation whose effects are out. The first clause is prevention;
the second keeps the predicate false by refusing to set the flag it reads,
which we do not count as prevention---a refused retraction is a basis someone
wished to withdraw after release, the ordinary rule that a committed
transaction cannot abort---and \cref{sec:l2-eval} counts its residue beside
the zero.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{What the detector decides.} A trace without
provenance fields cannot decide \cref{def:a3}. What it can decide is an
\emph{unsupported read}---a committed read of a value that no surviving
committed write produced at or before the read. The verified
\texttt{detect\_a3} decides exactly that screen, and its relation to
\cref{def:a3} is five mechanized facts (supplement \S\,G): it is not sound
in general (a value collision hides the aborted writer), it is sound under
value-unique writes, the hypothesis black-box isolation checkers also
need~\cite{Aphyr2020Elle,TanEtAl2020Cobra}, it holds along chains, it is not
complete, and it is blind to relabeled effects. We therefore claim
\cref{def:a3} only where the hypothesis holds, and check it: value-unique
writes hold by construction in the synthetic cascades and in all
\HypLiveLtwoSessions{} live replay sessions (\HypLiveLtwoCollisions{}
collisions), the two data sets the detector scores; they fail in
\HypCollisionCells{} cells of the pilot, refusal-study, occurrence-study and
one exec-ledger campaign, on none of which \A3 is scored. A recorder that stamps each written value with its
writer's operation identifier makes the hypothesis a system invariant.

\subsection{\A6: tool-effect reordering}
\label{sec:a6}
Each record also carries an issuance order $\fld{io}$ and a commit order
$\fld{co}$ over its external effects; $\ws$ and $\wv$ derive from $\fld{co}$.
\begin{definition}[Tool-effect reordering]
\label{def:a6}
$h$ exhibits \emph{tool-effect reordering} when for some $i$,
\[
  \begin{gathered}
  |h[i].\fld{io}|\ge 2,\quad h[i].\fld{co}\neq h[i].\fld{io},\\
  h[i].\fld{co}\ \text{is a permutation of}\ h[i].\fld{io}.
  \end{gathered}
\]
\end{definition}
The minimal witness (three states, one agent, two cells) is benign over store
cells, where writes to distinct cells commute; the predicate names an anomaly
only when each element of $\fld{io}$ is an external effect whose order an
observer can see. It over-reports on commuting effects and, requiring a
permutation, is silent on dropped and duplicated effects (\cref{sec:dup};
supplement \S\,Q).


\subsection{Snapshot insufficiency}
\label{sec:snapshot}
$L_1$ is not a snapshot guarantee: ``reads reflect memory at read time''
is the store axiom of \cref{thm:corc}, holds on every reachable log, and
TLC exhibits a history satisfying it on which \A1 fires
(\texttt{MC\_A1\_struct}; supplement \S\,B); LangGraph's superstep pins a
snapshot per node and has not obtained $L_1$ (\cref{sec:live-a1}).

\subsection{What the catalog leaves: the residue predicate}
\label{sec:residue}
Every predicate above is decided at or before an operation's commit, and a
discipline that excludes all four still leaves a harm the live study measures
in $1$--$65\%$ of sessions per family and arm on the API families
(\cref{sec:live-a1}): the
external effect a commit released is irreversible, while the state it was
derived from is ordinary revisable state that any later operation may
overwrite. This is not a concurrency anomaly. It fires in correct sequential
executions---book against the date known at commit, then be contradicted by
a legitimate revision---and classical isolation has no name for it because a
committed transaction's inputs are final by definition; the nearest notions
are view maintenance~\cite{GuptaMumick1995ViewMaint} with the view outside
the system, and the saga's compensation~\cite{GarciaMolinaSalem1987Sagas}. We
define it because an evaluation scored on outcomes needs a predicate for the
outcome's other half, and keep it outside the catalog and the lattice.

Each operation record carries a \emph{basis}, the (cell, value) pairs its
external effect was derived from, and an $\fld{obligation}$ flag. The basis
is recovered by \emph{argument provenance}: a read value that appears
verbatim among the effect's arguments is part of its basis, and nothing is
declared by the agent. This is why-provenance~\cite{BunemanEtAl2001Why}
restricted to copying, with its known limit: a value that
reaches the effect through a computation leaves no basis, so the extractor
under-reaches and never over-reports. In the model the basis is any subset
of the read values, so every extractor is covered.

\begin{definition}[Residue predicate]
\label{def:residue}
$h$ exhibits a \emph{superseded-basis effect} (\Rp{}; \texttt{NoA7} in the
artifact) when for some $i$, $h[i].\fld{externalized}$,
$\neg h[i].\fld{obligation}$, and for some $(c,v)\in h[i].\fld{basis}$ the
\emph{latest} write to $c$ committed after $h[i].\wt$ wrote a value other
than $v$:
\[
  \begin{gathered}
  \exists j:\ c\in h[j].\ws \;\wedge\; h[j].\wt > h[i].\wt\\
  \qquad\wedge\; h[j].\wv[c]\neq v\\
  \wedge\ \forall k:\ (c\in h[k].\ws \wedge h[k].\wt > h[i].\wt)\\
  \qquad\qquad\Rightarrow h[k].\wt \le h[j].\wt.
  \end{gathered}
\]
\end{definition}

On a workload that revises a cell once, \A1 and \Rp{} partition ``an effect
is out on a basis the state no longer holds'' by whether the superseding
write committed inside the operation's window or after its commit (with two
revisions one effect can fire both); a value overwritten and then restored
fires neither, and \Rp{} carries no agent conjunct because the obligation is
owed whoever supersedes. On the live data the partition is exact
(\cref{tab:live}): $A_1 + R = 100$ in every unguarded cell, and under $L_1$
the residue is entirely \Rp{}. The identity is not evidence for the
extractor: on a workload that copies the date verbatim into the booking it
holds whenever the extractor recovers the basis at all, and it recovers
\HypBasisRecovered/\HypBookings{} because the harness writes the read value
into the effect's argument; we keep it as a gate (\texttt{a7\_rescore
-{}-check}) and state the extractor's blind spot in \cref{sec:limitations}.
TLC's five-state witness is not an \A1 witness: an effect released with
basis $\{(c_1,\mathrm{NULL})\}$, then a commit of $c_1=v_1$.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{What remains after release (a remark, not a
result).} Under the \emph{open-world hypothesis}---the store accepts, at
any time after an effect $e$ with basis $B$ is released, a write to a cell
of $B$ by an operation that does not depend on $e$---two things are
immediate and fix what a discipline can promise. (i)~Some execution
supersedes $B$ while $e$ is out, because the write is admitted after a
release that cannot be recalled. (ii)~If obligations are never cleared,
$\neg R$ holds in every reachable state iff $e$'s obligation is set in the
same step as, or before, every step that completes the other conjuncts of
\cref{def:residue} (the superseding commit for a released effect; the release
for an effect superseded while held): \cref{def:residue} is ``superseded and
not owed,'' and a monotone flag set by the first such step stays set. So a
discipline can promise not a current basis but an obligation registered
atomically with the step that completes the witness, which the ledger model
of \cref{sec:ledger} does at both steps (the executable ledger clears a
discharged obligation; never-cleared obligations are the model's). Dropping
the hypothesis is not free: freezing the cells of $B$ until the effect is
acknowledged is a lock held past commit, priced by the pessimistic column of
\cref{tab:cost}; output commit (\cref{sec:a3}) holds effects only until their
\emph{causal} basis is externalized, a finite condition. What a registered
obligation buys in the world is a property of the compensator and is
measured, not proved (\cref{sec:ledger-world}).

\subsection{At-most-once effects, as a predicate}
\label{sec:dup}
The model externalizes an operation's effects once, at commit. LangGraph's
\texttt{interrupt} resumes a node by re-executing it from its start, so an
irreversible effect issued \emph{before} the gate is emitted again after it,
with different arguments when the shared value was revised during the gate
(supplement \S\,Q; \HypInterruptEmissions{} emissions of one pre-gate
effect in the probe). We state the hypothesis as a predicate over the
effect stream: $D$ holds when two
emissions carry the same node's effect, $D^{\neq}$ when they also differ in
their arguments. A five-action \TLA{} module (\texttt{Duplication.tla}:
issue, gate, resume-from-start, finish, revise) reaches $D$ in
\DupWitnessDepth{} steps and $D^{\neq}$ in \DupDivergentDepth{}; with an
idempotency key per (node, effect) TLC finds neither over \DupDedupStates{}
states exhaustively. $D$ stays outside the catalog and the lattice like
\Rp{}: its carrier, the effect stream, is disjoint from the four
disciplines', which neither create nor remove it. On the committed operation
records \HypDuplicateEffects{} of \HypExternalEffects{} externalized effects
were emitted twice (\texttt{hypothesis\_gates.py}, H5); no campaign of
those records drives LangGraph's \texttt{interrupt}, and the probe's two
emissions are in its own receipt, outside that count.

% ============================================================================
% sec/04-lattice.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{The lattice and one chain through it}
\label{sec:lattice}

A consistency model is a predicate over histories, characterized by the set of
anomalies it excludes~\cite{Adya1999}. With
$\mathcal{A}=\{A_1,A_2,A_3,A_6\}$ the design space is the Boolean lattice
$\langle 2^{\mathcal{A}},\subseteq\rangle$: sixteen points, bottom
$\emptyset$ admitting everything, top $\mathcal{A}$ admitting nothing
(drawn in supplement \S\,L). We name five points on one maximal chain,
\[
  L_0\equiv\emptyset,\;
  L_1\equiv\{A_1\},\;
  L_2\equiv\{A_1,A_3\},\;
  L_3\equiv\{A_1,A_3,A_6\},\;
  L_4\equiv\mathcal{A},
\]
chosen on operational grounds (\A1 first as the basic read--generate--commit
failure; \A2 last because registry stability across the operation lifetime
is the costliest requirement); the other
twenty-three linearizations are equally valid and the lattice, not the chain,
is primary. \Rp{} is not a generator: it is not an anomaly, and its answer,
the basis ledger $\ell$ of \cref{sec:ledger}, composes with any point.


\subsection{What is and is not a result}
The chain's inclusions are definitional and its strictness needs one witness
per step; neither is a result. What requires work is \emph{realizability}: a
runtime configuration that excludes exactly each point's anomalies, and for
the five named points a verified realizing runtime---established by bounded
model checking at two agents, two cells and two operations (three for \A3
and for the supersession class of \A1), one value for the witness half and
two for the safety half, bounds that exercise every minimal witness and
nothing longer; the unbounded statement exists only for the top point, as
the Verus product of \cref{sec:composition}.

\subsection{All sixteen points, mechanically; the ledger composes}
\label{sec:lattice16}
\texttt{Guarded.tla} extends the runtime with four commit-time disciplines,
each enabled by a Boolean constant and each acting on its own carrier: $G_1$,
read-set validation (an operation whose read set was overwritten after its
read may not commit); $G_2$, registry validation (a planned tool no longer in
the registry may not be dispatched); $G_3$, causal tracking with output commit
(an operation records the closure of committed writers whose values it read,
may not commit while any is aborted, externalizes only once its closure has
externalized, and once externalized is irrevocable; a retraction cascades to
every unexternalized dependent); $G_6$, commit-order sequencing (effects
externalize in issuance order). The carriers are disjoint---read set and write
times; registry and planned tool; closure and abort flags; issuance and commit
orders---so enabling one guard neither creates nor removes another anomaly,
and the matrix confirms it on every combination.

For each of the sixteen guard sets $S$ and each anomaly $a$, TLC checks
$R(S)\models\neg a$: for $a\in S$ the invariant must hold exhaustively, for
$a\notin S$ it must be violated. All \LatticeRuns{} runs match
(\texttt{lattice16.sh}; $|A|{=}2$, $|\fld{Cells}|{=}2$, $|\fld{Values}|{=}1$,
$\fld{MaxOps}{=}2$, symmetry on agents and cells; the $32$ safety runs are
exhaustive over \LatticeSafeStatesMin{}--\LatticeSafeStatesMax{} distinct
states). Because \textsc{CausalCascade} never ranges over cells, the sixteen
\textsc{NoA3} points are rechecked at $\fld{MaxOps}{=}3$ with one cell, where
a transitive cascade first becomes reachable:
\LatticeMaxOpsThreeRuns{}/\LatticeMaxOpsThreeRuns{} match. A
single-value domain makes \cref{def:a1}'s value conjunct vacuous on non-null
reads; the safety half re-run at $|\fld{Values}|{=}2$ on the current model
stays safe (\LatticeVtwoOutcome), and a targeted check shows the
non-null-read witness class---the live study's supersessions---needs both a
second value \emph{and} a third operation (supplement \S\,L), so at
$\fld{MaxOps}{=}2$ the \textsc{NoA1} safety verdicts exclude only the
cold-start class\ifnum\LatticeAOneMaxOpsThreeAvailable=1 ; the sixteen
\textsc{NoA1} points are therefore rechecked at $\fld{MaxOps}{=}3$,
$|\fld{Values}|{=}2$ and one cell, where that class is reachable
(\texttt{lattice16\_a1\_maxops3.sh}; supplement \S\,L):
\LatticeAOneMaxOpsThreeShort; the two-cell universe at that depth did not
close in $25$ minutes ($72$M states) and the verdict covers one cell\else;
their recheck at $\fld{MaxOps}{=}3$, $|\fld{Values}|{=}2$, where that class is
reachable, is \Pending{formal/tla/lattice16\_a1\_maxops3.sh}\fi. Every point
is thus inhabited by a runtime that excludes exactly its avoidance set
(soundness only), at these bounds.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{The ledger composes.} \texttt{Guarded5.tla} adds a
fifth constant $G_7$, the basis ledger of \cref{sec:ledger}, which refuses
nothing and writes only the basis and obligation fields.
\texttt{lattice32.sh} runs all \LatticeThirtyTwoRuns{} combinations of the
five constants against the four anomalies and \Rp{} at the same bounds. The
$64$ anomaly cells with $G_7$ on return the verdicts of \texttt{lattice16.sh}
one for one, so the ledger disturbs no point of the lattice; \Rp{} is
reached without $G_7$ in a five-state trace at every point and excluded
with $G_7$ at every point (exhaustive; \NoAsevenStates{} distinct states at
the bottom point, \NoAsevenStatesGthree{} with output commit); no run
mismatches its expectation.

% sec/05-mechanization.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Mechanization}
\label{sec:mechanization}

Three tools carry the development, at assurance levels \cref{tab:assurance}
labels claim by claim. TLC~\cite{Lamport2002SpecifyingSystems} checks the
witnesses, the lattice and ledger
matrices and the fifteen \cref{thm:corc} verdicts (bounded, exhaustive
within bounds). TLAPS~\cite{CousineauEtAl2012TLAPS} proves \cref{thm:corc}
(\TlapsCommitOrder{} obligations), a coherence check of the chain definitions
(\texttt{Hierarchy.tla}, \TlapsHierarchy{}) and a same-agent exclusion lemma
(\texttt{A1LowerBound.tla}, \TlapsLowerBound{}); only the first is a
substantive result. Verus~\cite{Lattuada2023Verus} carries the substantive
mechanization: \VerusCurated{} obligations (\VerusFull{} counting four helper
files), zero errors, no \texttt{assume} or \texttt{admit} in any proof body,
regenerated by \texttt{verus\_count.sh} against a pinned build. Of these,
\VerusLoadBearing{} bear a deployed or measured artifact's safety claim and
\VerusScaffold{} are scaffolding (superseded refinements, the $A_4$ model,
the model-only Verus mirror of \texttt{Guarded.tla}); supplement \S\,S
inventories every file.

\subsection{Verified detectors, and one transcription gap}
\label{sec:verus-detectors}
The four detectors are verified sound \emph{and} complete against Verus
\texttt{spec} restatements of the \TLA{} predicates (\VerusDetector{}
obligations). \texttt{detect\_a1} is a two-sided equivalence whose
completeness half strengthens each of three nested loop invariants to carry
``no witness in the prefix searched.'' \texttt{detect\_a2} and
\texttt{detect\_a6} are single-record equivalences; \texttt{detect\_a3} is
exact for the flat-trace residue of \cref{sec:a3}.

The restatement is hand-written and checked by inspection, which found a
gap we state: the Verus \texttt{OpRecord} carries no
agent field, so its \A1 predicate omits the different-agent conjunct of
\cref{def:a1}, and it decides ``no witness'' when a read value is absent,
where the \TLA{} compares against $\mathrm{NULL}$. Neither matters on the
histories the model admits.

\begin{lemma}[Same-agent exclusion]
\label{lem:same-agent}
Under per-agent non-overlap and read-before-commit, if
$c\in h[i].\rs\cap h[k].\ws$ and $h[i].\rt<h[k].\wt<h[i].\wt$, then
$h[i].\fld{agent}\neq h[k].\fld{agent}$.
\end{lemma}
The lemma is step $\langle2\rangle2$ of the TLAPS proof of \cref{thm:corc}
and the content of \texttt{A1LowerBound.tla}. It makes the Verus predicate
and \cref{def:a1} coincide on every history satisfying the model's invariants;
on a history that violates non-overlap the Verus predicate is strictly
stronger---it over-reports, the safe direction for a detector. The
instrumentation records a value for every read cell, so the absent-value case
does not arise: across all \HypRecords{} committed operation records there
is no read cell without a value and \HypSameAgentOverlaps{} same-agent
overlapping windows (\texttt{hypothesis\_gates.py --check}).

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Which bytes score \A1.} Agreement of three
transcriptions on committed data is testing, not equivalence; an earlier
version scored its tables by a hand-written port with such a gate. The
scorer now takes its \A1 verdict from
\texttt{detect\_a1} itself: the verified exec-mode file
(\texttt{lib\_detect\_a1\_exec.rs}, $9$ obligations, sound and complete
against \texttt{is\_a1\_witness}, the gated restatement's conjuncts over
(cell, value) pairs; agreement checked by inspection and on every cell by the
gate) is compiled by plain cargo into the runtime crate byte-identical to the
verified source, behind a $60$-line wrapper that interns cell names and values
injectively, drops a duplicated declared cell (precondition \texttt{wf\_hist})
and encodes a read cell without a value as a sentinel no record can carry---the
one unverified piece between the verified bytes and a string-valued record,
trust-base item (xii). The port is kept beside it and the binary refuses a
trace on which the two disagree. \ifnum\VerifiedScorerAvailable=1 Every published \A1 cell was
rescored through the verified bytes: \VerifiedScorerCells{} cells,
\VerifiedScorerTraces{} traces, \VerifiedScorerProblems{} disagreements with
the manuscript's counts and with an independent Definition-1 implementation
(\texttt{verified\_scorer\_gate.py}; receipt committed; its first pass had
failed on a reader defect the gate caught, supplement \S\,W.5).\else Every
published \A1 cell was rescored through the verified bytes
(\texttt{verified\_scorer\_gate.py}: \VerifiedScorerCells{} cells,
\VerifiedScorerTraces{} traces, each verified count equal to an independent
Definition-1 count), but the gate exited non-zero on \VerifiedScorerProblems{}
per-cell \texttt{refusals.jsonl} sidecars of the refusal study that it
handed the analyser as op-records (a reader defect, like the one its first
pass caught; supplement \S\,W.5), so its clean receipt is \Pending{runner
step 14 rerun} and until then the tables also stand on the gated port.\fi

\subsection{$L_1$: two concurrent stores under a verified lock}
\label{sec:l1}
Three abstract state machines---pessimistic locking, serializable snapshot
isolation (SSI), and default snapshot isolation with its no-write bypass---are
proved \A1-free under sequential interleaving ($40$ obligations; default-SI
conditionally, on workloads with no read-only commit, which is the residual
\cref{sec:pilot} measures). The deployed stores do not rest on these: each is
an exec-mode store whose only access path is \texttt{vstd::rwlock::RwLock},
the reader--writer lock implemented and verified inside the Verus standard
library, carrying the store's invariant as the lock predicate
(\texttt{lib\_si\_concurrent.rs}, $18$; \texttt{lib\_pess\_concurrent.rs},
$31$; no axiom, no \texttt{external\_body}). The invariant entails the absence
of any stale-generation window in the store's own record stream, under any
interleaving of threads. Theorem L1j (\texttt{lib\_si\_projection.rs}, $3$)
transports that guarantee to the detector's history for \emph{faithful}
emitters---one \texttt{OpRecord} per store record, cells a subset of the
record's. The default-SI \texttt{tick} path is the standing counterexample to
faithfulness and is exactly the $\PilotSiEditResidual\%$/$\PilotSiTriageResidual\%$
residual of \cref{sec:pilot}. Four earlier refinements ($86$ obligations,
two axioms) relate the abstract machines to representations shipped
\emph{before} the verified-lock rebuild and bear no deployed claim; refining
the two concurrent stores to the abstract machines is the open proof step
(a bounded simulation over $506{,}880$ transitions finds no counterexample;
supplement \S\,H).

\subsection{$L_2$: output commit}
\label{sec:l2}
The $L_2$ model (\texttt{lib\_l2\_safety.rs}, $33$) carries per transaction a
predecessor set recorded as a causal closure at read time. A commit is valid
iff the read set is fresh against the committed store and every predecessor
is committed and unaborted; an abort cascades to every transaction whose
closure holds the aborter; effects leave by a separate \texttt{externalize}
step enabled only once the closure has externalized; an externalized
transaction cannot abort. Theorem $L_2$h: no state satisfying the inductive
invariant $\fld{inv\_output\_commit}$ contains a witness of \cref{def:a3}
(\texttt{lemma\_l2\_reachable\_no\_a3}), under arbitrary interleaving of the
six steps; the closure invariant makes the one-level cascade provably
sufficient for the transitive case. Theorem $L_2$i: every committed read has
a surviving producer of exactly its value, committed no later than the read.
Theorem $L_2$j (\texttt{lib\_l2\_projection.rs}, $3$): a faithful emission of
an $L_2$-supported state contains no residue witness. The exec runtime
(\texttt{lib\_l2\_exec.rs}, $73$ end to end, $40$ net of the re-included
model) refines the model per transition with zero added axioms; every
mutating entry point checks its own guard, so the guarantee survives proof
erasure under plain \texttt{cargo}, and the file the reference runtime
compiles is byte-identical.

\subsection{$L_3$: sequencing, and $L_4$: validation at dispatch}
\label{sec:l3}\label{sec:l4}
Theorem $L_3$g (\texttt{lib\_l3\_sequencer.rs}, $5$): a commit-order
sequencer that externalizes effect $k$ once effects $0..k$ have completed
yields an externalization time monotone in issuance index for \emph{every}
completion schedule, hence no \A6, without forbidding concurrency; a
reversed schedule exhibits \A6 unsequenced and none sequenced. The saga
model (\texttt{lib\_l3\_safety.rs}, $6$) is the strict-serialization special
case and composes with $L_2$ on disjoint carriers. The exec sequencer
(\texttt{lib\_l3\_exec.rs}, $7$) is verified and compiled byte-identical into
the runtime crate; \ifnum\ToolNodeVerifiedAvailable=1 its measurement
(\cref{sec:a6-eval}) runs those bytes behind a $70$-line JSON bridge that
releases real effects in the order they decide (trust-base item xiii).\else
the measurement of \cref{sec:a6-eval} published here ran a dependency-free
twin, a gap the trust base records; the replay through the verified bytes
behind a $70$-line JSON bridge is \Pending{runner step 15}.\fi

Resolving a planned tool against a registry snapshot does not prevent \A2: a
snapshot changes what an operation resolves, not the tool its call reaches
(Theorem $L_4$d, a concrete execution). The $L_4$ model
(\texttt{lib\_l4\_safety.rs}, $9$) reads \A2 from a \emph{dispatch record}
and validates at dispatch---place the call only if the planned tool is still
live with the pinned signature---so registry churn cannot create \A2, a
property of the dispatch. The exec dispatcher (\texttt{lib\_l4\_exec.rs},
$11$) refuses a revoked or re-signed tool and dispatches an untouched one
(\cref{sec:l4-eval} measures those bytes). Validation and dispatch are one
atomic step of a registry the runtime owns; a remote tool server
(MCP~\cite{AnthropicMCP2024}) offers no such atomicity, and the
check-then-call gap there is narrowed, not closed.


\subsection{The basis ledger}
\label{sec:ledger}
The ledger $\ell$ answers \Rp{} and composes with any discipline; the live
study runs it over the application-layer $L_1$ (\cref{tab:live}; the arm
over the verified store is \ifnum\LiveRunsAvailable=1 the
\emph{ledger-verified} row of the replicated run\else\Pending{runner step
17}\fi). Its model
(\texttt{lib\_l5\_ledger.rs}, \LfiveVerus) carries the
store's committed values and, per effect, its basis map, an externalized
flag and an obligation flag. Three steps: \emph{register} commits an effect
with its basis (released at once, or held under output commit; if released
at once on a basis the store already disagrees with, the obligation is set
at commit); \emph{write} installs a value and marks, in the same step, every
externalized effect whose basis holds that cell with a different value;
\emph{externalize} releases a held effect and marks it if its basis is stale
by then. The invariant $\mathit{inv\_l5}$---every externalized effect with a
stale basis carries an obligation---is preserved by each step (the write
step is the load-bearing case: the cell that became stale is the cell the
step marked, and any other stale cell was stale before), and the ledger theorem:
no state satisfying $\mathit{inv\_l5}$ contains a witness of \cref{def:residue}.
The model's witness is the state form---an externalized, unowed effect whose
basis the store now disagrees with---which \cref{def:residue}'s witness
implies (a differing latest post-commit writer is a differing current
value), so excluding it excludes the definition's witness and an effect
released on a basis already stale at commit. Non-vacuity in both directions:
the unguarded write reaches \Rp{} in two steps from an effect derived from
an unwritten cell, and under the ledger the same write registers the
obligation while a write that restores the value leaves no witness. The
ledger refuses nothing; refusing a stale-basis release, or a hold deadline,
is a policy layered on top. The invariant
speaks of records: a compensator that reports a discharge while a stale
confirmation stays active satisfies it vacuously, which is what
\cref{sec:ledger-world}'s world check found. The model is \LfiveVerusIntro{} under the pinned
build; its obligations count as scaffolding (\VerusScaffold{} with it)
because the executable ledger of the live study is harness code, not these
bytes; it registers the obligation at the superseding write---inside the
store lock in the shipped design, a path only probe 304's receipts record
(\cref{sec:ledger-world}); the probe-303 rows carry no registration
field---and runs the compensation in that writer's thread after the lock is
released. An exec ledger
(\texttt{lib\_l5\_exec.rs}, supplement \S\,S) carries the bookkeeping
alone---which effects a write obliges (total: no superseded basis without an
obligation), what a compensator's verdict does, what is open---behind a JSON
bridge; the store hook, the interning and the compensator stay in the trust
base. \ifnum\LedgerWorldExecAvailable=1 The ledger arm of
\cref{sec:ledger-world} rerun with its bookkeeping in those bytes (proofs
erased by cargo; server \texttt{\LWexecServerSha}; three families): world
wrong \LWexecLedgerWorldWrongRange, none \LWexecLedgerWorldNoneRange,
agreement $\LWexecLedgerAgree$, the bytes' open set equal to the shadow's in
every session---the same bookkeeping, the residue the world's. \fi\ifnum\LfiveExecAvailable=1 The file is verified
(\LfiveExecVerus; outside the \VerusCurated/\VerusFull{} totals, which
predate it) and byte-identical in the runtime.\else Its verification
under the pinned build is \Pending{runner step 7, pinned build}; until that
receipt the bookkeeping is a port.\fi

\subsection{Composition}
\label{sec:composition}
\texttt{lib\_consistency\_lattice.rs} takes the product of the $L_2$, $L_3$
and $L_4$ states over their disjoint carriers and proves that the conjunction
of the three invariants excludes \A1 (image), \A3, \A6 and \A2 jointly, with
three non-interference lemmas ($53$ obligations, $48$ of them the re-verified
components). The ledger's carrier (basis, obligation) is disjoint from all
three; adding it to the product is the same construction, not in this version.

\subsection{Trust base}
\label{sec:trust}
``Zero \texttt{assume}, zero \texttt{admit}'' is a statement about proof
bodies. What is trusted, in full: (i) Verus, its SMT backend and
\texttt{vstd}, including \texttt{group\_hash\_axioms} for the
\texttt{HashMap}-backed exec states; (ii) two axioms in the four superseded
refinements (string injectivity, null sentinel) and (iii) three
\texttt{external\_body} stubs for a \texttt{parking\_lot::Mutex}, neither
bearing a deployed claim since the verified-lock rebuild; (iv) the hand
restatement of the \TLA{} predicates in Verus, with the gap of
\cref{sec:verus-detectors} stated and gated; (v) the hand-written ports that score \A2, \A3 and \A6 (\A1 is the
verified detector, item xii), gated against the verified predicates on every
committed cell; (vi) for $L_3$, the inspection-level correspondence between the exec
artifact and the measured twin; (vii) the \texttt{pub} fields of the exec
runtimes, which a caller can write around the API; (viii) the marshalling
that flattens the version map into the verified validation gate, and the
interning bijection and abort counter outside the verified store; (ix) the
Python and Rust instrumentation that produces every \texttt{OpRecord},
including the read sets it declares---the largest item: a detector is as
good as the read set it is handed, and S-Bus~\cite{Khan2026SBus}, our own
read-set reconstruction from HTTP traffic, finds $74\%$ of reads on a
SWE-bench workload invisible to that surface; at the store boundary the
declared read set equals the keys read (gate H6: \HypUndeclaredReads{}
undeclared store reads over \HypObservedRecords{} records of probes 303 and
304), while a value reaching the model through prompt context neither
instrument sees. (x) the
provenance correspondence between the two formalizations of \A3, which agree
only under value-unique writes (checked on every scored trace); (xi) in the
live study's verified arm, the $100$-line JSON bridge and the harness's
interning of cells and values; (xii) the $60$-line interning wrapper between
the verified \A1 detector and a string-valued record
(\cref{sec:verus-detectors}); (xiii) the $70$-line bridge between the verified
$L_3$ sequencer and the tool executor (\cref{sec:a6-eval}); (xiv) for the
exec ledger of \cref{sec:ledger}, the bridge with its Python shadow, the
store hook that calls it inside the lock, and the compensator. No safety-bearing
proof invokes a writes-as-function-of-reads assumption, and the development
carries no probabilistic obligation.

% ============================================================================
% sec/06-evaluation.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Evaluation}
\label{sec:eval}

Three questions. \textbf{RQ1} For each discipline, what does it prevent, and
what harm remains once it has? \textbf{RQ2} What does prevention cost, per
unit of work the discipline actually delivered? \textbf{RQ3} Where do the
structural preconditions of the anomalies occur in deployed code? A verified
arm's zero is a theorem, so every zero below is reported beside a count that
reads no runtime flag.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Provenance.} Every table cell maps to a committed
trace directory and a scorer in the artifact; \texttt{reproduce.sh} rescores
them offline under an implementation of \cref{def:a1} independent of the
verified detector and the Rust port, and fails on any disagreement.
Model-driven cells are one run each at the stated $n$ unless replicated; a
regeneration reproduces the protocol and the intervals, not the sessions.
Intervals are exact Clopper--Pearson unless stated.

\subsection{RQ1 on a live runtime: what $L_1$ removes and what it leaves}
\label{sec:live-a1}
The scenario of \cref{sec:intro-example} runs on a released LangGraph
runtime (\texttt{langgraph} 1.2.0) with live models. Two compiled graphs
share one \texttt{BaseStore}, the framework's cross-thread memory, subclassed
only to stamp a logical clock at \texttt{get}/\texttt{put} under one lock,
the one instrumentation (\texttt{BaseStore} offers no version or
compare-and-set). A flight agent reads the trip date,
drafts a reservation over a model call and puts the booking; a date agent,
started by the harness \LiveOffsetMs{}\,ms after the flight agent's read, reads
the date, asks its model for a one-week revision and puts it. Three modes:
\emph{vanilla}; \emph{validated}, the $L_1$ discipline at the application
layer (re-read before \texttt{put}, discard the generation and regenerate on
change); and \emph{sequential}, a control: the date agent runs after the
flight agent commits. \LiveNPerCell{} sessions per cell on three model
families. The booking's date is the value the flight agent read; the models
draft the texts and set the latencies; model output is scored only in the
materiality study below. Each session records the \A1 verdict, the
regenerations and the booking's and final dates (\cref{tab:live}).

\begin{table*}[t]
\centering

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{The window is open and the framework does not
close it.} On both API models the runtime committed the stale booking in
every session in which the revision landed inside the window
($\LiveGptVanillaAOne\%$ and $\LiveHaikuVanillaAOne\%$), the detector fired
with the exact temporal witness of \cref{def:a1}, and nothing in the
framework blocked, validated or reported any of them.
\phantomsection\label{sec:materiality}A value mismatch may be lexical: in
the live study the stale date reached the booking text in $150$ of $152$
firings (September run); in a controlled triage task, varying only the shared
value an agent read changed its decision in $150/200$ flagged mismatches
($75.0\%$ $[68.4, 80.8]$), the paper's only value-staleness estimate; on the
pilot's firings materiality is $43/43$ under an LLM judge, $15$ of them
hand-coded blind (supplement \S\,N).
Behind a request-serializing local server the open-weights family's
revision landed after the commit in $99/100$ sessions (the one firing had the
longest pinned interval, $\LiveLlamaPinnedMax$\,s): a property of the serving
configuration, not of the model.

\caption{Probe 303, offset sweep: the live \A1 rate without a discipline (dashed) and the residue \Rp{} that read-set validation leaves (solid; equal to booking $\neq$ final, since \A1 is $0$ there) as the date agent's start is delayed; revision probability $0.5$, $n=100$ per point. \label{fig:offset}

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{What $L_1$ removes, and what it does not.}
Read-set validation removed every firing ($0/300$) at one discarded
generation per realized conflict (\LiveGptRegen{} and \LiveHaikuRegen{} per
$100$ sessions, never two in one session). It did not make the booking agree
with the final date: in $\LiveGptValidatedWrong$ and
$\LiveHaikuValidatedWrong$ of $100$ sessions the revision landed after the
commit---the record is current at its commit point, as \cref{thm:corc}
says, and the booking is stale against a state that did not
yet exist: a post-commit supersession, the blind overwrite of \cref{sec:a1},
outside every isolation discipline, reached only by compensation or by
holding the effect until its basis is final. The \emph{sequential} control,
clean under every commit-time predicate, delivers the wrong booking in
$100/100$ sessions for the same reason; the predicate alone would credit
$L_1$ with a correctness it does not provide.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{\Rp{} decides the residue on the records.}
The \Rp{} column of \cref{tab:live} is decided on the emitted records alone,
from the date value in each booking's arguments, and fires in exactly the
sessions whose booking disagrees with the final date and \A1 does not
($A_1+R=100$ in every unguarded cell; \texttt{a7\_rescore.py -{}-check};
\cref{sec:residue} says what the agreement rests on). Two further arms run on three API families
(haiku, gpt-4o-mini and deepseek-chat through its OpenAI-compatible
endpoint). The application-layer arm's validating re-read and its
\texttt{put} are two store calls, so it is check-then-act, not an atomic
commit: a revision landing between them would commit stale, and would be an
\A1 witness on the record, whose read time is the validating read's; none did
in the $3{,}900$ sessions of this study that ran over it ($2{,}700$
validated, $1{,}200$ ledger), which bounds the window without closing it. In the \emph{verified} arm the $L_1$ decision is taken
inside the verified store's lock through the bridge of \cref{sec:trust}: \A1
fires in $0/300$ sessions and the residue is $2$, $51$ and $52$ of $100$
against $12$, $51$ and $63$ for the application-layer arm of the same run. In the \emph{ledger}
arm a superseding put registers the obligation and a compensator issues a
replacement booking: $0/100$ wrong on all three families ($5$, $45$ and $53$
obligations, each discharged by one compensation generation), at the price
of $L_1$'s discarded generations plus one per obligation and a stall of the
superseding writer, in whose put the compensation runs
(\cref{sec:ledger-world}).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{What a difference between arms can mean, and
what the replication says.} The four arms of \cref{tab:live}'s October run
were sequential blocks of $100$ sessions, and the $L_1$ residue is the
share of revisions that landed after the commit---a race between two
provider latencies plus the fixed offset---so a latency drift between
blocks moves it without any change of discipline: the two $L_1$ arms on
haiku, one discipline decided in two places, differ at $12$ vs.\ $2$ of
$100$ (Fisher exact $p=\ArmFisherHaiku$). The attributable design
interleaves the arms per session in a seeded order, with fresh seeds per run;
\cref{tab:liveruns} reports \LiveRunsHaiku{} such runs of five arms per family
(the fifth, \emph{ledger-verified}, is the ledger over the verified store).
Paired per session, the validated arm alone left the
residue in \RunsPairedDiscordantHaiku{} sessions of $300$ on haiku (McNemar
exact $p\RunsMcNemarHaiku$; difference $\RunsDiffHaiku$ points, Newcombe 95\%
$\RunsDiffCIHaiku$), \RunsPairedDiscordantGpt{} on gpt-4o-mini
($p\RunsMcNemarGpt$; $\RunsDiffGpt$ $\RunsDiffCIGpt$) and
\RunsPairedDiscordantDeepseek{} on deepseek-chat ($p\RunsMcNemarDeepseek$;
$\RunsDiffDeepseek$ $\RunsDiffCIDeepseek$): no arm difference, bounded at
four points on haiku and about ten elsewhere; the sequential-run haiku
difference did not replicate. \A1 is $0$ in the $900$ October-sequential and
$3{,}600$ interleaved sessions run under $L_1$ at this offset. The run, not
the arm, is where the residue moves: against the sequential run (different
date, seeds and design; nominal $p$-values that bound the drift without
attributing it), vanilla \A1 is $94$ vs.\ $\RunsHaikuVanillaAOnePooled$ on
haiku ($p\RunsVsSeqHaikuVanillaAOne$) and $44$ vs.\
$\RunsGptVanillaAOnePooled$ on gpt-4o-mini ($p\RunsVsSeqGptVanillaAOne$;
deepseek-chat $p\RunsVsSeqDeepseekVanillaAOne$), the $L_1$ residue $12$ vs.\
$\RunsHaikuValidatedRPooled$ and $51$ vs.\ $\RunsGptValidatedRPooled$
($p\RunsVsSeqHaikuValidatedR$, $p\RunsVsSeqGptValidatedR$; deepseek-chat
$p\RunsVsSeqDeepseekValidatedR$), and the three interleaved runs of one arm
differ on deepseek-chat alone (validated residue $54$/$73$/$58$, $\chi^2$
$p\RunsHeteroDeepseekValidatedR$; the pooled intervals treat the $300$
sessions as one sample, which that test qualifies). The ledger rows do not
move: $\RunsHaikuLedgerWrongPooled$ wrong bookings in both ledger arms on
every family (exact 95\% interval $\RunsHaikuLedgerWrongCI$)---on the store,
the qualification \cref{sec:ledger-world} measures.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{The offset sweep.} \Cref{fig:offset} varies the
race instead of fixing it: the date agent starts $0$, $150$, $500$ or
$1{,}500$\,ms after the flight agent's read and revises in about half the
sessions ($p=0.5$), $n=100$ per point on the three API families; at $0$\,ms
the harness imposes only the order, and the models' latencies decide where
the revision lands. Without a
discipline \A1 fires in $39$--$46$ of $100$ sessions at $0$\,ms and in $0$ at
$1{,}500$\,ms, where every revision lands after the commit, while the
booking is wrong in every revised session at every offset ($44$--$51$).
Under $L_1$ the curve inverts: the residue is $2$--$17$ at $0$\,ms and
$40$--$52$ at $500$--$1{,}500$\,ms, the discarded generations falling from
$34$--$49$ to $0$. Predicate and residue are the two halves of one quantity,
the revision rate, split by where the revision lands relative to the commit;
what the remark of \cref{sec:residue} says must remain after release does
remain under every commit-time discipline.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Superstep.} A second graph places a writer and a
summarizer of one reducer-declared channel in one Pregel
superstep~\cite{MalewiczEtAl2010Pregel}; the barrier hands both the snapshot
at superstep entry, so in $300/300$ sessions the summary names the
destination the writer had changed: the snapshot-insufficiency shape of
\cref{sec:snapshot} observed live, reported as documented BSP semantics, not
a rate (the census finds the shape in two of \CensusSites{} public sites,
\cref{sec:prevalence}; under a completion clock \cref{def:a1} fires in
\SuperstepGpt, \SuperstepHaiku, \SuperstepLlama{} of $100$).


\subsection{The ledger in a world: a consumer, a cancellation, a compensator that fails}
\label{sec:ledger-world}
The ledger arm's $0/300$ is a statement about the final store, under a
compensator that could not fail, a workload that revised once and no reader
of the first booking. None of that holds in a world, so we measure the same
discipline against a simulated one (probe 304): the booking is an
irreversible effect in an in-process service that stays active until
cancelled; a \emph{consumer}---a downstream reader that sends the
itinerary---reads the active bookings a uniform
$0$--$\LedgerWorldConsumerMaxMs$\,ms after the first release (a harness
parameter); the date
agent revises \emph{twice}, $150$\,ms after the flight agent's read and
$800$\,ms after its first put returns; and the world refuses a cancellation
or a rebooking with probability $\LedgerWorldPCancelFail$ each, in which case
the obligation stays registered and open, retried at the next supersession
and only then. Three arms share every session's seed and run interleaved:
$L_1$ alone; $L_1$ with the ledger and a compensator that drafts the
replacement, cancels every active stale booking, rebooks, and discharges only
when every step succeeded---it runs inside the superseding put, in the date
agent's thread, so in the ledger arms that put returns only after the
compensation's model call; and the same without the cancellation, the
compensator of \cref{tab:live} seen from the world. Scored at session end
\emph{in the world}: sessions with an active booking whose date is not the
final one (\emph{wrong}), with two (\emph{double}), with none (\emph{none}),
whose consumer acted on a stale date (\emph{c.\,stale}), and---the column no
store can show---in which the ledger reports every obligation discharged
\emph{and} the world holds exactly the final booking.
\ifnum\LedgerWorldAtomicAvailable=1
\Cref{tab:ledgerworld} is the result on three families, the obligation
registered inside the store lock (the shipped design; supplement \S\,W.4 has
the after-put run and the first compensator). Under $L_1$ alone the second
revision lands after the regenerated commit in \LWatomicValidatedRRange{}
sessions of $100$, so \Rp{} fires, store and world are wrong, and the consumer
acted on a stale date in the same sessions: with two revisions the residue of
\cref{tab:live} is nearly total. The ledger registers an obligation in every session whose
booking committed before a revision landed (all but
\LWatomicLedgerNoObligationRange{} of $100$) and \Rp{} is $0$ in all $300$
ledger sessions, by construction (the obligation record is what the predicate
reads)---and that is where its guarantee ends. At session end the world holds
a stale booking in \LWatomicLedgerWorldWrongRange{} sessions per family
(beside the new one in \LWatomicLedgerDoubleRange{} of them: the cancellation
was refused) and no booking in \LWatomicLedgerWorldNoneRange{} (the rebooking
was refused). Those shares are the injected refusal rate of
$\LedgerWorldPCancelFail$ seen from the other side, less what a later
supersession retried: on haiku only the second revision lands after the
commit (one obligation in \LWatomicHaikuLedgerOneObligation{} of $100$,
$\LWatomicHaikuLedgerRetries$ retries); on deepseek-chat both do in
\LWatomicDeepseekLedgerBothRevisionsAfterCommit, two obligations in
\LWatomicDeepseekLedgerTwoObligations{} and $\LWatomicDeepseekLedgerRetries$
retries of one left open. The consumer, reading
within $1.5$\,s of the first release, acted on a booking the final date
contradicts in \LWatomicLedgerConsumerStaleRange{} sessions, as under $L_1$
alone; its \texttt{at\_ms} against the records places those reads: in
\LWatomicLedgerConsumerStaleBeforeRevisionRange{} no revision had landed
(the booking was current); in
\LWatomicLedgerConsumerStaleInsideCompensationRange{} the latest revision's
compensation had not completed---its model call is that window; in
\LWatomicLedgerConsumerStaleRefusedCancelRange{} the stale booking stood
because the world had refused its cancellation (obligation open); in
\LWatomicLedgerConsumerStaleCurrentThenSupersededRange{} the consumer saw
the then-current booking, which a later revision replaced---a reader that has
read cannot be corrected, only notified. The column is the compensator's
ordering: the first compensator, which cancelled before drafting, left the
consumer a stale booking in \LWfirstLedgerConsumerStaleRange{} and none at
all in \LWfirstLedgerConsumerNoneRange{} of $100$ (supplement \S\,W.4).
Without the cancellation the world
is wrong in \LWatomicLedgerNoRollbackWorldWrongRange{} of $100$ and
double-booked in \LWatomicLedgerNoRollbackDoubleRange{}: a ledger that does
not withdraw the effect moves nothing in the world.

The last column is what the ledger is for. In the sessions whose every
obligation it reports discharged (\LWatomicLedgerCleanSessionsRange{} of
$100$, of which \LWatomicLedgerCleanVacuousRange{} registered nothing) the
world holds exactly the final booking in every one
(\LWatomicLedgerCleanWrongRange{} exceptions), and in every session whose
obligation it reports open (\LWatomicLedgerOpenSessionsRange{}) the world is
wrong or empty (\LWatomicLedgerOpenRightRange{} exceptions): report and world agree in
$\LWatomicLedgerAgree$ sessions. That is a property of this compensator, not
of the ledger---what a correct compensator owes by construction, which the
world check establishes and the records cannot---and the first run of the
same probe (supplement \S\,W.4) shows the difference: its compensator, which
drafted the replacement \emph{between} cancelling and rebooking, reported
discharge with the original still active in
$\LWfirstHaikuLedgerCleanWrong$, $\LWfirstGptLedgerCleanWrong$ and
$\LWfirstDeepseekLedgerCleanWrong$ of the sessions it called clean; report
and world agreed in $\LWfirstLedgerAgree$. \Rp{} was $0$ in both runs: it
cannot tell the two compensators apart; the world does, and the lesson is the
remark of \cref{sec:residue}: a registered obligation buys exactly its
compensator's truthfulness. Two further runs bound the rest.
\ifnum\LedgerWorldAvailable=1 With the obligation registered \emph{after}
the put returned (the design until 2026-10-16; another day, unpaired) every
world column overlaps the atomic run's (world wrong
\LWLedgerWorldWrongRange{}, none \LWLedgerWorldNoneRange{}, consumer stale
\LWLedgerConsumerStaleRange{}, agreement $\LWLedgerAgree$)---a null by
construction, since the probe injects no fault in that gap, which the code
closes unmeasured; the compensation it precedes takes
\LWatomicLedgerCompLatencyMedianSRange{}\,s (median), and that is where the
residue is. \fi\ifnum\LedgerWorldWideAvailable=1 With the consumer
reading up to $5$\,s after the first release it acts on the stale booking in
\LWwideLedgerConsumerStaleRange{} of $100$ (world wrong
\LWwideLedgerWorldWrongRange, none \LWwideLedgerWorldNoneRange, agreement
$\LWwideLedgerAgree$): the column measures the reader's window against the
compensation's latency, not the ledger.\fi
