\subsection{RQ1 across three disciplines: the AutoGen pilot}
\label{sec:pilot}
An instrumentation layer exposes a two-phase \texttt{begin}/\texttt{commit}
API to LLM-driven agents under AutoGen~\cite{WuEtAl2023AutoGen}; the Rust
runtimes of \cref{sec:l1} sit behind it. Three workloads span the space from
concurrent to sequential: \emph{edit-review} (two agents both read
\texttt{doc} before either writes---overlap forced), \emph{plan-execute}
(strictly sequential---overlap forbidden) and \emph{triage} (a three-agent
pipeline whose overlap is left to the models); gpt-4o, $100$ sessions per
cell, three runtimes (run \texttt{\CostRun}; the table is in supplement
\S\,M). \A1 fires in $100\%$ / $0\%$ / $48\%$ of vanilla
sessions on the three workloads (triage replicated three times:
\PilotTriageRates; pooled \PilotTriagePooled), in $0\%$ everywhere under
pessimistic locking, and in $\PilotSiEditResidual\%$ / $0\%$ /
$\PilotSiTriageResidual\%$ under default snapshot isolation, whose residual
is its no-write bypass (a read-only commit is never validated: the
unfaithful emitter of Theorem L1j; SSI mode closes it). Both endpoints are
engineered, so this is a sensitivity check of the detector and the runtimes
on model-driven traces, not a prevalence; every edit-review witness is a
cold-start read, unlike the live study's supersessions. One harness defect
bears on the prices: until 2026-10-17 the pilot's tools told the model
\texttt{COMMITTED} whatever the runtime did (records unaffected: a dropped
operation emits nothing); they now return \texttt{REFUSED} under a recorded
policy (\texttt{-{}-on-abort drop|retry}), and the cells here are reported
as run with the old tools. Pessimistic locking
reaches $0\%$ on edit-review by refusing the reviewer's operation in
$99/100$ sessions---the anomaly is absent because the work is---and on
triage by dropping one of three agents in $97/100$; the sessions that are
\emph{complete and clean} (no witness, every role that commits in the
unguarded cell also commits) are the number to read a prevention claim
against, and on triage they favor the unguarded baseline ($52$) over
default-SI ($45$) and locking ($3$); whether a dropped contribution or a
stale one is worse is the task's call, which is why \cref{sec:cost} prices
prevention per sound committed operation. A Claude Sonnet~4.5 rerun of the
vanilla column with operation records reproduced the two determinate cells
(edit-review $100/100$, plan-execute $0/100$) and found the triage cell void:
the reporter's prompt supplied no bug, gpt-4o invented one and Sonnet wrote
``no bug description provided'' in $100/100$ sessions. The pilot now seeds
the reporter with a concrete bug per scenario and records the protocol beside
each cell; the gpt-4o run predates this and is reported as run.
\ifnum\PilotClaudeAvailable=1 Rerun so and scored by the verified detector,
the Sonnet cells fire \A1 in \VSClaudeEditReviewVanilla{} /
\VSClaudePlanExecuteVanilla{} / \VSClaudeTriageVanilla{} sessions
unguarded, \VSClaudeEditReviewPess{} / \VSClaudePlanExecutePess{} /
\VSClaudeTriagePess{} under locking and \VSClaudeEditReviewSI{} /
\VSClaudePlanExecuteSI{} / \VSClaudeTriageSI{} under default SI. The records
say why the two cells differ from gpt-4o's: on Sonnet the triager writes a
priority in $0$ of the $600$ triage sessions of the six cells on record here
outside the refusal study's locking arms, and the engineer a resolution in
$0$ (supplement \S\,M lists them; gpt-4o: $44$ and $41$ of $100$), so no
write lands in a reader's window and the triage cell cannot fire; and the Sonnet reviewer
writes its review in $50$ of the scored run's $100$ unguarded sessions
($47$--$48$ in two later runs) and otherwise commits read-only, the
no-write bypass of Theorem L1j that default SI never validates---$50/100$
against gpt-4o's $5/100$ (SSI mode closes it).
Which operations a model issues is part of the workload (supplement
\S\,M).\else Its \A1 rate and the six guarded Sonnet cells are scored
by the verified detector; the fold withholds them until the scorer gate's
receipt is clean, \Pending{runner step 14 rerun} (supplement
\S\,M).\fi{}

\subsection{RQ1 for $L_2$: output commit, synthetic and live}
\label{sec:l2-eval}
Three arms run the same $1{,}000$ seeded schedules at nominal chain depths
$2$, $3$ and $5$: a root commits, a chain of dependents reads the previous
cell and commits, one committed transaction is put under review and retracted
or approved ($771$ of $1{,}000$ retract), and in a quarter of the
retractions ($199$) the review arrives after that transaction has released. The \emph{verified} arm is \texttt{l2\_exec.rs};
the \emph{unguarded} arm validates reads, releases at commit and flags only
the aborted transaction; the \emph{cascade} arm releases at commit and
cascades the abort. Each trace is scored under \cref{def:a3}, under the
surviving-dependent predicate, and by \emph{exposed effects}, effects out at
the end although the review asked to withdraw their basis, which reads no
flag (full table: supplement \S\,P). \Cref{def:a3} fires in
\LtwoDefThreeBaseline{} of $1{,}000$ schedules per depth under both
baselines and in none under the verified arm; the cascade arm satisfies its
own surviving-dependent predicate in every scenario while exposing exactly
as many effects as no discipline at all (\LtwoExposedBaseline{}), because it
flagged \LtwoRelabeled{} dependents \emph{after} their effects were out---a
cascade without output commit moves flags, not effects. The verified arm is
not zero on exposed effects: \LtwoExposedVerified{} effects in $199$
scenarios per depth, exactly those in which the review arrived after the
release and the retraction was refused. The figures decompose by arithmetic
($1{,}538 = 402 + 572 + 564$ at depth 2): the first term no runtime can
remove; the second any runtime that declines to release an operation under
review avoids; the third ($37$--$46\%$ of the unguarded exposure) is output
commit's own contribution, at a cost in delay, not liveness. The quarter of
late reviews is a harness parameter, so the overall exposure reduction
($73$--$74\%$) is that parameter seen from the other side, not a finding.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Live verdicts.} A plan--execute--revise workload
driven by real models (planner, executor acquiring the planner as
predecessor, supervisor deciding retraction) was recorded for $200$ sessions
on each of three families and replayed through the three arms. Retraction
rates varied sharply ($1/200$, $3/200$, $62/200$ on claude-haiku-4-5,
gpt-4o-mini, Llama-3.2), so only the pooled statement is made: in every one
of the \LiveLtwoRetractions{} retracted sessions the two baselines left a
\cref{def:a3} witness and the verified runtime released no executor effect
on a retracted plan (pooled release rate $89.0\%$); $1{,}000$ further haiku
sessions produced no retraction, leaving the hold as the discipline's only
observable cost there (median $687$\,ms, p90 $789$\,ms per kept effect). The
zero is structural; the \A3 hazard is the backbone's retraction rate.

\subsection{RQ1 for $L_3$: a released tool executor reorders}
\label{sec:a6-eval}
LangGraph's \texttt{ToolNode} dispatches a turn's tool calls concurrently
(\texttt{asyncio.gather}), collects results in input order and does not
serialize side effects (its tracker records a user unable to obtain
sequential execution for stateful tools, langgraphjs \#861). We gave three
backbones a natural prompt over five order-dependent infrastructure tools
whose docstrings state their dependencies, took the model-emitted call list as
$\fld{io}$, and dispatched it through the released \texttt{ToolNode} and
through the $L_3$ sequencer.
Two things are real---whether a backbone batches an order-dependent
pipeline into one turn, and the executor---and per-tool latency is modeled
(uniform $10$--$200$\,ms); the replay against real effects below removes
the model. The finding that survives the modeling is structural (table in
supplement \S\,P): gpt-4o-mini batches an order-dependent pipeline on
$\ToolNodeGptBatched/30$ turns, the full five-tool pipeline on
$\ToolNodeGptFull$ of them, and \texttt{ToolNode} commits
$\ToolNodeGptReordered$ of the $\ToolNodeGptBatched$ out of order ($0$ under
$L_3$); Llama-3.2 batches $\ToolNodeLlamaBatched/30$ and reorders
$\ToolNodeLlamaReordered$; claude-haiku-4-5 never batches ($0/30$); and the
transcript a developer inspects shows the correct order in all
\ToolNodeBatchedTurns{} batched turns while the external state does not in
\ToolNodeReorderings{} of them.
Calls emitted in one turn are, by the provider's contract, calls the model
declared parallelizable, so the defect is shared between a backbone that
misjudged independence and an executor that cannot notice; the $L_3$
sequencer makes effect order independent of the backbone. Replayed five
times each through \texttt{ToolNode} against real effects---a WAL-backed
SQLite database and a local HTTP service, with their own latency---the $33$
recorded batches fire baseline \A6 in \ToolNodeRealSqlite{} replays (SQLite) and
\ToolNodeRealHttp{} (HTTP): the reorder is a property of concurrent dispatch
under any real latency, not of the distribution we drew. The $L_3$ arm of
that replay issued the calls one at a time, which cannot reorder and
measures nothing; \ifnum\ToolNodeVerifiedAvailable=1 the arm that counts
dispatches the batch concurrently through the same executor and releases
each real effect only when the verified sequencer (\cref{sec:l3}) releases
it. In a second, separate replay of the same $33$ batches (runner step 15,
with its own baseline arm, which differs from the first run's because
concurrent dispatch is nondeterministic): \A6 in \ToolNodeVerifiedSqlite{}
replays (SQLite; baseline \ToolNodeVerifiedBaselineSqlite{} in that run) and
\ToolNodeVerifiedHttp{} (HTTP; baseline \ToolNodeVerifiedBaselineHttp).\else
the arm that counts---concurrent dispatch through the same executor, each
real effect released only when the verified sequencer of \cref{sec:l3}
releases it---is \Pending{runner step 15}.\fi

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{$L_4$: validation at dispatch.}\label{sec:l4-eval}
One seeded schedule per run pins a tool, then revokes or re-signs random
tools; three dispatchers act on one registry. Over $1{,}000$ runs at
registry widths $2$, $4$ and $16$ the churn touched the planned tool in
\LfourPhantom{} runs, and in exactly those both live and snapshot resolution
called a phantom (Theorem $L_4$d, measured) while the verified dispatcher
called none and refused exactly those; the informative figures are the
hazard rate and the refusal cost, the zero is Theorem $L_4$b executing.

\subsection{RQ2: what prevention costs}
\label{sec:cost}
The pilot driver captures per-call usage and wall-clock for every session of
run \texttt{\CostRun} (gpt-4o, $900$ sessions) and of its Claude Sonnet~4.5
counterpart (May 2026, no operation records; \cref{sec:pilot}), at list
price at the time of the run. Per session, snapshot isolation's token
cost is not separable from the unguarded runtime's on either provider
(overlapping intervals in every cell); pessimistic locking's is
provider-dependent (at most $1.6\times$ on gpt-4o and $2.2\times$ on Claude,
both on triage). A per-session denominator credits a discipline for work it
declined to do, so \cref{tab:cost} re-prices the same run per committed
operation and per \emph{sound} committed operation---one that is not the
stale reader of any \cref{def:a1} witness.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\caption{Token cost of prevention under three denominators, gpt-4o, run
\texttt{\CostRun}. Per sound committed operation is the price we recommend
reading. Underlying counts (commits / sound): edit-review vanilla $212/112$,
pessimistic $101/101$, SI $110/105$; triage vanilla $335/283$, pessimistic
$205/205$, SI $268/256$; plan-execute $\approx200/200$ for all three.}
\label{tab:cost}
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrr@{}}
\toprule
Workload & Discipline & per session & per commit & per sound commit \\
\midrule
edit-review  & pessimistic & $+3.8\%$  & $+117.9\%$ & $+15.1\%$ \\
edit-review  & SI          & $+0.6\%$  & $+94.0\%$  & $+7.4\%$  \\
plan-execute & pessimistic & $+1.7\%$  & $+1.2\%$   & $+1.2\%$  \\
plan-execute & SI          & $+0.4\%$  & $+0.4\%$   & $+0.4\%$  \\
triage       & pessimistic & $+42.7\%$ & $+133.2\%$ & $+97.0\%$ \\
triage       & SI          & $+0.5\%$  & $+25.6\%$  & $+11.1\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Parity does not survive the change of denominator: snapshot isolation costs
$+7.4\%$, $+0.4\%$ and $+11.1\%$ per sound committed operation, and
pessimistic locking's triage penalty is $+97\%$ rather than $+43\%$.
Overhead tracks realized contention: edit-review, which aborts hardest, pays
most; plan-execute, which never aborts, pays at most $+1.7\%$ under any
denominator. A separate $155$-session paired sweep on gpt-4o-mini (fan-in $2$--$16$,
realized abort rates $0.09$--$0.93$) resolves the mechanism: snapshot isolation carries no fixed overhead (intercept $+0.05\%$,
CI $[-1.5,+1.7]$) and pays $1.074\times$ $[1.052, 1.096]$ a first-attempt
generation per abort, because the retry carries the conflict into its prompt
(supplement \S\,M).

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{Wall-clock, with its confound.} The nine cells of
a run ran one after another, so a cell difference carries the provider's
latency drift. Plan-execute, on which no runtime aborts, is the control; its
``overheads'' are $-4.5\%$ and $-4.9\%$ on gpt-4o (Holm-corrected
permutation $p=0.014$, $0.002$) and $+13.5\%$ for SI on Claude ($p=0.001$):
drift, whose largest magnitude is the run's null band. Outside it: pessimistic
locking at $+16.7\%$ (edit-review) and $+34.9\%$ (triage) on gpt-4o and
$+109.5\%$ (triage) on Claude, $7.75$ model calls per session against
$4.00$. Snapshot isolation is inside the band in every cell on both
families---not zero: the design resolves nothing below about $5\%$ on gpt-4o
or $14\%$ on Claude (supplement \S\,O). Probe~302 removes the confound by
interleaving the runtimes per session.
\ifnum\WallclockPairedClaudeAvailable=1 On Claude Sonnet~4.5 its paired
per-session overheads (mean, bootstrap 95\% CI) are triage pessimistic
\WallclockPairedClaudeTriagePess{} and SI \WallclockPairedClaudeTriageSI,
with the plan-execute control at \WallclockPairedClaudePlanExecutePess{} and
\WallclockPairedClaudePlanExecuteSI{} (supplement \S\,O for every

\ifnum\RefusalClaudeAvailable=1 With the tools telling the truth (Claude

\subsection{RQ3: where the preconditions occur}
\label{sec:prevalence}
To put a denominator under the structural precondition of \A1 in
the framework we studied---a reader and a writer of one last-value channel
released into the same superstep---we analyzed public code; the answer is
\emph{none confirmed}, by an instrument blind to about half the repositories.
\emph{Frame}: $60$ GitHub
repositories per star band matching \texttt{langgraph} (Python; forks and
the framework's own organization excluded), a seeded draw from the API's
$500$ most recently updated; all $180$ were read (supplement \S\,Q).
\emph{Instrument}: a static front end that recovers from the syntax tree each
\texttt{StateGraph} site, the node pairs one fork releases into a superstep,
and each node's read and written keys; what it cannot resolve is
\emph{unknown}, never guessed (validated against execution on eight runnable
fixtures). Of the $180$, \CensusRepos{} contain a visible graph, holding
\CensusSites{} sites, \CensusSequential{} ($96\%$) of them sequential;
shares below are of the sampled repositories, unweighted across bands.
Fifteen sites in \CensusParallelRepos{} repositories ($11.2\%$, Wilson 95\%
$[6.4, 19.0]$) contain a co-scheduled pair; none is a confirmed
reader/writer pair on a last-value channel (repository-level upper limit
$3.8\%$); two are such pairs on a reducer channel (the
parallel-agents-on-\texttt{messages} idiom: the snapshot-insufficiency shape
\cref{sec:live-a1} runs live, not \A1's); eight have an unresolved side,
bounding the susceptible share at $8.2\%$ $[4.2, 15.3]$ of repositories.
Counting every site the front end cannot settle---an unresolved side,
\texttt{Send} fan-out (seven sites, the construct outside the model,
\cref{sec:model}) or routing it cannot resolve---gives $94$ sites in $50$
repositories ($51\%$): the instrument's reach, where nothing is confirmed
or excluded, not a bound. An enriched population---the
\CensusErrorStringRepos{} third-party repositories whose issues quote
LangGraph's co-superstep writer/writer error, so demonstrably ran parallel
writers---has parallel graphs in $12$ of its $26$ with a visible graph but
again no confirmed site in $391$. Of five deployed designs we probed, four
close the window by construction; LangGraph's superstep channels and
cross-thread store leave it open, where \cref{sec:live-a1} measures it
(supplement \S\,Q).

\subsection{Threats to validity}
\label{sec:threats}
\emph{Construct.} \A1 tracks the window, not the harm; \cref{tab:live}
reports both and \Rp{} names the gap. The basis is argument provenance,
verbatim and not semantic; whether a booking that disagrees with a later
revision is harm is the application's call, and we count it because the user
asked for one trip. \emph{Internal.} The live rates are latency races, $n=100$ per cell,
single runs in \cref{tab:live} and \cref{fig:offset} and three interleaved
runs in \cref{tab:liveruns}; the application-layer $L_1$ arm is
check-then-put (\cref{sec:live-a1}); the sequential wall-clock cells carry a
run-order confound we bound rather than remove (\cref{sec:cost}); the
pilot's prices depend on what a model does with a refusal, its own or a
peer's broadcast one (\cref{sec:cost}); RQ2 is priced in tokens for $L_1$
only---$L_2$ in hold latency, $L_4$ in refused dispatches, $L_3$ unpriced
(the sequencer adds no model call); the L2 live replay rests on $66$
retractions, $62$ from one family. \emph{External.} Three API families and one local; one framework
driven live; toy workloads of two to three agents. \emph{Reproducibility.}
Every cell is committed with its traces and rescored by the verified detector
and an independent implementation of \cref{def:a1}; one September run has a
run-time manifest, two reconstructed ones that say so.

% ============================================================================
% sec/07-related.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Related work}
\label{sec:related}

\subsection{Classical consistency and isolation}
The database isolation
hierarchy~\cite{BerensonEtAl1995,Adya1999,AdyaLiskovOneil2000} is the direct
ancestor: we adopt its anomaly-based method over a different operational
model, and the SSI line~\cite{FeketeEtAl2005MakingSI,CahillRohmFekete2008SSI,PortsGrittner2012}
is the reference for our SSI runtime. Commitment ordering~\cite{Raz1992CO}, Weikum and Vossen's
commit-order-preserving
serializability~\cite{WeikumVossen2001} and Kung and Robinson's backward
validation~\cite{KungRobinson1981OCC}---the read-set validation at commit of
Silo~\cite{TuEtAl2013Silo} and TicToc~\cite{YuEtAl2016TicToc}, which $L_1$
is---are what \cref{thm:corc} mechanizes at the level of values read (the
value-level statement differs from the conflict-level one exactly at the
overwrite-and-restore shape of \cref{sec:a1}, the ABA
problem~\cite{Michael2004Hazard}, whose classical remedy is the version tag of
requirement (i)); the application-layer arm of \cref{sec:live-a1} is a chopped
transaction~\cite{ShashaEtAl1995Chopping} with a check-then-act
window~\cite{BishopDilger1996TOCTOU}; ACIDRain~\cite{WarszawskiBailis2017ACIDRain}
is the precedent for scoring isolation anomalies by the harm they cause; view
equivalence~\cite{Papadimitriou1979} is where the value-level and
conflict-level notions meet, and \cref{def:corc} is a client-centric,
state-based statement of the kind Crooks et al.\ give for isolation
levels~\cite{CrooksEtAl2017Seeing}. Highly available
transactions~\cite{BailisEtAl2014HAT} classify levels by admitted anomalies,
the method our lattice borrows; Cerone et al.~\cite{CeroneEtAl2015Framework}
give the axiomatic framework our predicates specialize;
\emph{robustness}~\cite{BernardiGotsman2016Robustness} is what the census
asks statically. Compensation descends
from sagas~\cite{GarciaMolinaSalem1987Sagas}, its formal
treatment~\cite{KorthLevySilberschatz1990Compensating},
ConTracts~\cite{ReuterWachter1991ConTract} and Gray and
Reuter~\cite{GrayReuter1993}; recoverability and strictness are
Hadzilacos~\cite{Hadzilacos1988Reliability} and Bernstein et
al.~\cite{BernsteinHadzilacosGoodman1987}. Irrevocable
transactions~\cite{WelcEtAl2008Irrevocable} and transactional
boosting~\cite{HerlihyKoskinen2008Boosting} met the same irreversibility in
transactional memory; output commit is the dual choice. Commutativity-based
control~\cite{Weihl1988Commutativity} orders only what does not commute: an
\A6 discipline over commutativity classes would pay the sequencing cost only
where it buys something.
The basis of \cref{def:residue} is why-provenance~\cite{BunemanEtAl2001Why}
restricted to copying; exactly-once stream
processing~\cite{AkidauEtAl2013MillWheel} holds side effects at a
transactional sink until the input is durable, the industrial precedent for
output commit. The pessimistic/optimistic choice is STM's
contention-management problem; cost-aware level selection is consistency
rationing~\cite{KraskaEtAl2009Rationing} and
TxnSails~\cite{ZhouEtAl2025TxnSails}.

\subsection{Concurrency control and shared memory for LLM agents}
A 2025--2026 line builds mechanisms for this regime, mostly unverified; this
paper is one point in it, not its origin. Twenty of the 2026 works cited are
preprints at the time of writing, cited for the designs they describe; no
claim of ours rests on their results. Agentic
transactions~\cite{SunEtAl2026AgenticTx} recast ACID as four semantic
guarantees for one long-horizon agent and measure task success, with no
history predicate, model or proof; our catalog is the anomaly-level account
such guarantees would be checked against. CoAgent~\cite{LyuEtAl2026CoAgent} states
the regime in our three terms and proposes an advisory protocol in which a
notified agent repairs its own plan, correct conditional on a model judgment
measured at a $5\%$ failure rate; it reports the same lock/OCC cost
inversion as \cref{sec:cost}. STORM~\cite{LiuEtAl2026STORM} is read validation ($L_1$)
deployed per file; ATCC~\cite{ZhouEtAl2026ATCC} and GoEX~\cite{PatilEtAl2024GoEX}
impose a remedy from the runtime. MemTX~\cite{LiEtAl2026MemTX} is the closest
design to our $L_2$: staged writes under snapshot isolation, irreversible
tool calls gated on current belief, cascading repair on retraction, two
invariants checked by bounded enumeration ($5.5$M states), five backbones; it
does not state the predicates it excludes, verify the runtime, or price
prevention per unit of sound work. Yang et
al.~\cite{YangEtAl2026Position} argue that coordination failures attributed
to communication are concurrency failures; governed shared
memory~\cite{MargalitEtAl2026Governed} and transactive
memory~\cite{Transactive2026} address provenance, supersession and scoping
without treating the generation window as a transaction.
TOKI~\cite{Wang2026TOKI} is the closest formal treatment of supersession:
contradiction resolution in agent memory as write-time concurrency control,
with a soundness proof for a bitemporal operator family; it governs which
belief a memory keeps, where \Rp{} governs what an effect already out still
owes.
SAFEFLOW~\cite{LiEtAl2025SAFEFLOW} adds information-flow labels and
write-ahead rollback to transactional execution, and
Cordon~\cite{ChenEtAl2026Cordon} stages external actions in an effect outbox
released after whole-workflow validation---output commit at task
granularity, without a model of concurrent tasks or a proof.
StateFuse~\cite{VolkovEtAl2026StateFuse} keeps conflicting observations
visible under CRDT merging, orthogonal to exclusion like CodeCRDT; Yu et
al.~\cite{YuEtAl2026ExecConsistency} ask whether consistent shared memory
suffices for an agent's task duties, a question above our history level.
SagaLLM~\cite{GengChang2025SagaLLM}, Atomix~\cite{MohammadiEtAl2026Atomix}
and CodeCRDT~\cite{Pugachev2025CodeCRDT} are placed on the lattice informally
in supplement \S\,D (none reaches $L_4$; \S\,V compares the nearest systems
dimension by dimension). S-Bus~\cite{Khan2026SBus} (the author's prior work)
recovers implicit read sets for one contract, the natural supplier of
trust-base item (ix); frameworks~\cite{LangGraph2026,WuEtAl2023AutoGen}
supply substrate, not contract.


\subsection{Commit-time guards, retraction, and after commit}
\label{sec:related-residue}
The 2026 work on agent effects acts at the commit boundary:
CommitGuard~\cite{SantosGrueiro2026CommitGuard} licenses a durable effect
only by a witness still fresh at commit; Zheng et
al.~\cite{ZhengEtAl2026Stale} show that freshness guards of our $L_1$ kind
block $92$--$95\%$ of benign infrastructure races and argue for semantic
commit predicates; EffectMatch~\cite{ZhangEtAl2026EffectMatch} validates
persistent outcomes against what was approved; versioned
execution~\cite{ZhangEtAl2026Revisable} revokes an obsolete execution's
authority to publish. MemTX~\cite{LiEtAl2026MemTX} alone looks past commit:
on an explicit \emph{retraction} it walks a derivation DAG to executed tool
actions and records each as compensated or leaked. None treats the ordinary
case \cref{tab:live} measures---no retraction, no race, a legitimate later
write---or says what is achievable there; \cref{sec:residue} says an
obligation, registered at the superseding write. Zheng et al.'s precision
objection is met at the basis, as far as the basis is recovered: a basis
predicate ignores writes the effect never depended on.

\subsection{Effect order, durability, verification, taxonomies}
Parallel function calling is pursued deliberately
(LLMCompiler~\cite{KimEtAl2024LLMCompiler}, asynchronous
calling~\cite{GimEtAl2024AsyncLM}), the ordering of effects left to the
runtime; \A6 is a wrong independence judgment seen at the effect level
(\cref{sec:a6-eval}). Durable-execution
engines~\cite{BurckhardtEtAl2021Durable} and exactly-once workflow
systems~\cite{SkiadopoulosEtAl2022DBOS} govern whether an effect is
\emph{repeated} after a crash (the property $D$ of \cref{sec:dup} names),
output commit whether it may \emph{leave} while its basis can be withdrawn;
the two compose. Rollback recovery discharges duplicates by identity because
replay is deterministic, a premise agent runtimes
break~\cite{ZhengEtAl2026ACRFence}.
IronFleet~\cite{HawblitzelEtAl2015IronFleet} and
Perennial~\cite{ChajedEtAl2019Perennial} verify running systems by layered
refinement, the template for the open step of \cref{sec:l1}; ours add
verified bytes executed in a measurement. Anvil~\cite{SunEtAl2024Anvil}
verifies in Verus controllers that act on possibly stale cluster state,
liveness included, which we verify nowhere. Jepsen~\cite{Kingsbury2013Jepsen} and the black-box
checkers~\cite{TanEtAl2020Cobra,Aphyr2020Elle} (successors in supplement
\S\,R) decide isolation levels over database histories with unverified
analyzers under a uniqueness assumption we share (\cref{sec:a3}); ours
decide a far simpler property, verified, in the tradition of runtime
verification~\cite{BauerLeuckerSchallhart2011}.
MAST~\cite{CemriEtAl2025MAST} catalogs cognitive and coordination failures;
its traces cannot show \A1 either way (supplement \S\,C).
Agentproof~\cite{XavierEtAl2026Agentproof} analyzes agent graphs statically,
as the census does; MCMAS~\cite{LomuscioQuRaimondi2017MCMAS} verifies
task-level and strategic properties.

% ============================================================================
% sec/08-limitations.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Limitations and future work}
\label{sec:limitations}

\noindent\emph{The predicates are coarser than the phenomena.} \A1
over-reports in one shape (a value overwritten and restored inside the
window; \HypRestored{} of \HypAOneWitnesses{} witness pairs on the live
records) and under-reaches the harm by the post-commit supersession \Rp{}
names (\cref{tab:live}); \A6 fires on commuting effects and is blind to
dropped and duplicated ones.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{\Rp{} is not an anomaly, and its basis is
syntactic.} \Rp{} fires in $100/100$ sequential sessions because the
workload revises the date in every session; \cref{tab:live} establishes that
the harm an isolation discipline cannot reach is exactly this predicate, not
that concurrency causes it. Its basis is argument provenance: a booking
derived from the date by computation has an empty basis and is invisible, so
the \Rp{} column is a lower bound (coverage \HypBasisRecovered/\HypBookings{}
here, the date being a structured argument); free-text effects need a
declared or taint-tracked basis.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{A refused retraction is not a prevented anomaly.}
Output commit makes \cref{def:a3} unreachable partly by refusing to retract
an operation whose effects are out, which leaves the world as it was and is
counted (\cref{sec:l2-eval}); the exposure--latency curve between harness and
live driver is unmeasured.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Verification is relative to the trace, and the
triangle closes on one system only in part.} Trust-base item (ix) is the
largest, measured only at the store boundary: a value that reaches the model
through prompt context is invisible, and our own read-set reconstruction finds
most reads invisible on a realistic workload~\cite{Khan2026SBus}. \ifnum\VerifiedScorerAvailable=1 The \A1
verdict is now the verified bytes behind an interning wrapper; the \A2,
\A3 and \A6 verdicts remain ports gated against their specifications.\else
The \A1 verdict is the verified bytes behind an interning wrapper, its gate
receipt pending; \A2, \A3 and \A6 remain ports gated against their
specifications.\fi{} The refinement from the two concurrent stores to
their abstract machines is open (a bounded simulation is evidence, not
proof); the application-layer $L_1$ arm is check-then-put, its window bounded
by $3{,}900$ sessions, not closed (\cref{sec:live-a1}); the $L_2$--$L_4$ exec
artifacts are sequential objects a
multi-threaded caller must serialize; the ledger arm's executable is harness
code\ifnum\LfiveExecAvailable=1{} except its bookkeeping (\LfiveExecVerus)\fi, its
Verus model scaffolding. Liveness is verified nowhere.

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Prevalence is not established, and the census
points the other way.} The census bounds a precondition in public code
(none confirmed; routing unresolved in about half the repositories) and is
silent on private deployments, run frequency and the cross-thread store.

deployment are the empirical follow-up; the store refinements, the formal

% ============================================================================
% sec/09-conclusion.tex
% ============================================================================
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

What commit-time isolation leaves is not an anomaly: an effect out on a
basis later writes revise, which under an open-world store can only be owed,
and an obligation is worth exactly what its compensator does.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{What an agent framework should provide.} Four
requirements follow from the measurements, each priced.
(i)~\emph{A versioned store with compare-and-set.} LangGraph's
\texttt{BaseStore} has neither, so $L_1$ cannot live inside the framework;
with it, read-set validation removes every stale commit at one discarded
generation per realized conflict, and per sound commit optimistic validation
costs $+0.4$--$11.1\%$ where locking costs up to $+97\%$.
(ii)~\emph{An obligation hook on the write path}, atomic with the write,
and compensators tested against the external system, not the records: \Rp{} was $0$ under a
compensator that misreported, and the stale effect stands for the
compensation's model call (median \LWatomicLedgerCompLatencyMedianSRange{}\,s).
(iii)~\emph{A sequencer in the tool executor} for calls emitted in one turn
unless declared commuting: in the replay in which the verified sequencer
committed none out of order, the released \texttt{ToolNode} committed
\ToolNodeVerifiedBaselineSqlite{} and \ToolNodeVerifiedBaselineHttp{}
against real effects. (iv)~\emph{Validation at dispatch}
for tools of a mutable registry, and an idempotency key on resumed effects:
snapshot resolution called a phantom tool in every run the churn touched; a
re-executed node emitted its effect twice.
